Restrictions of Pfaffian Systems for Feynman Integrals
Vsevolod Chestnov, Saiei J. Matsubara-Heo, Henrik J. Munch, and Nobuki Takayama

Abstract
This work studies limits of Pfaffian systems, a class of first-order PDEs appearing in the Feynman integral calculus. Such limits appear naturally in the context of scattering amplitudes when there is a separation of scale in a given set of kinematic variables. We model these limits, which are often singular, via restrictions of 𝒟 \mathcal{D} -modules. We thereby develop two different restriction algorithms: one based on gauge transformations, and another relying on the Macaulay matrix. These algorithms output Pfaffian systems containing fewer variables and of smaller rank. We show that it is also possible to retain logarithmic corrections in the limiting variable. The algorithms are showcased in many examples involving Feynman integrals and hypergeometric functions coming from GKZ systems. This work serves as a continuation of Chestnov:2022alh .



1
Introduction

The evaluation of Feynman integrals in perturbative quantum field theory is a central, albeit challenging, problem.
A widely used approach is to obtain a set of differential equations (DEQs) whose solution yields the Feynman integrals in question.
In this setting, one considers a vector
I
of unknown functions, dubbed the
master integrals
, which depend on a collection of kinematic variables
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
, such as the masses and momenta involved in a scattering experiment.
Using the shorthand notation
\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}
, the DEQs obeyed by the master integrals take the form

\displaystyle\partial_{i}I=P_{i}\cdot I\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,m\,,

(1)

where each
P_{i}
is an
r\times r
matrix with entries being rational in
z
.
This is called the
Pfaffian system
for
I
.
Using (
1
), the unknown vector
I
can be analytically expressed in terms of iterated integrals
bams/1183539443
;
Duhr:2014woa
, or computed by numerical integration
Liu:2022chg
;
Hidding:2020ytt
;
Armadillo:2022ugh
for fixed values of
z
.

In practical calculations pertaining to high-energy physics, there is often a
scale separation between the
z
variables. Namely, one variable, say
z_{1}
,
is
small
compared to the rest (or large, in which case
1/z_{1}
is
small). A severely incomplete list of examples includes the large top mass
expansion
Davies:2021kex
, the small transverse momentum expansion

Alasfar:2021ppe
, the multi-Regge limit
Caron-Huot:2020vlo
,
the soft limit in gravity
Parra-Martinez:2020dzs
,
various kinematic regions in effective field theories such as the soft-collinear effective theory
Becher:2014oda
,
and the computation of boundary conditions using expansion by regions
Dulat:2014mda
in the threshold
DiVita:2014pza
or massless
Mastrolia:2017pfy
limits.
There are also interesting limits such as threshold expansions
Lee:2018ojn
;
Beneke:1997zp
and limits of the
x
-parameter in the Simplified Differential Equations approach
Syrrakos:2023syr
;
Papadopoulos:2014lla
which involve special kinematic configurations rather than scale-separated variables. We can model these
situations as a (typically singular!) limit
z_{1}\to 0
on a Pfaffian system.
Starting from (
1
), the aim of this article is
then to derive a
simpler
Pfaffian system which holds in the limit (see
also
(
haraoka2020linear
, Chapter 12)
and
Bytev:2022tav
for similar
approaches). By simpler, we mean that the new Pfaffian system has a smaller
rank than
r
, and it depends on one variable less, because
z_{1}
decouples
from the system.

Our algorithms are rooted in the theory of
restrictions
(in the sense
that
z_{1}
is being “restricted” to
0
), which is historically tied to the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
As a supplement to studying DEQs from the viewpoint of analysis,
\mathcal{D}
-modules leverage the algebraic structure of differential operators.
When a particular
\mathcal{D}
-module is
holonomic
, meaning that it is associated to a system of DEQs with a finite number of solutions, one can take advantage of the remarkable work by M. Kashiwara
kashiwara-1975
and subsequent series of works (see
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
and references therein), which generalized the theory of regular connections by P. Deligne
Deligne
.
This is now established as the theory of regular holonomic systems
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
.
In the context of holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules, we can construct bases of differential operators, obtain relations between basis elements, count the number of solutions to DEQs, and more.
This naturally links
\mathcal{D}
-modules to Pfaffian systems for Feynman integrals.
Indeed, in this work, restrictions are studied from two complementary points of view: one at the level of Pfaffian systems, and the other at the level of
\mathcal{D}
-modules.

We take our inspiration from a particularly well-studied holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module: the GKZ hypergeometric system
GKZ-1989
- though, as we show, the algorithms presented here also apply beyond this case.
In the GKZ framework
Nasrollahpoursamami:2016
;
delaCruz:2019skx
;
Klausen:2021yrt
;
Klausen:2019hrg
;
Klausen:2023gui
;
Tellander:2021xdz
;
Pal:2021llg
;
Pal:2023kgu
;
Ananthanarayan:2022ntm
;
Feng:2022kgh
;
Feng:2022ude
;
Feng:2019bdx
;
Zhang:2023fil
;
walther2022feynman
;
Dlapa:2023cvx
;
Agostini:2022cgv
;
Munch:2022ouq
;
Klemm:2019dbm
;
Bonisch:2020qmm
, one generalizes parametric representations of a Feynman integral to include extra variables, such that now
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})
where
N\geq m
, with the inequality generally being strict.
On one hand, this means that there are more variables to manage.
On the other hand, additional structure arises which can be facilitated in the computation of Pfaffian systems.
In particular, one can immediately write a collection of (higher-order) differential operators which annihilate the generalized Feynman integral.
These operators generate the GKZ
\mathcal{D}
-module.
Using the fast algorithm of
Hibi-Nishiyama-Takayama-2017
, it is possible to construct a basis of operators (the so-called standard monomials) for the GKZ
\mathcal{D}
-module, which translates into a basis of integrals for the Pfaffian system.
Encoding algebraic relations between the generators of the GKZ system into a special matrix, called the
Macaulay matrix
, one can readily obtain the Pfaffian matrices associated to this basis too.
This strategy for computing Pfaffian systems was carried out in the manuscript
Chestnov:2022alh
, and we regard this work as continuation of the latter.

After having made use of this GKZ technology, one seeks to dispense with the auxiliary
z
variables to match with the proper Feynman integral.
In other words, one asks for a
restriction
to the case
N=m
, such that the number of variables agree.
In this work, we present two strategies with different benefits that solve the restriction problem.

Pfaffian-level restriction

In this strategy, we assume that a Pfaffian system (
1
) is given.
For instance, the system could have been derived via integration-by-parts
(IBP) following Laporta’s algorithm
Laporta:2001dd
,
intersection theory
Mastrolia:2018uzb
;
Frellesvig:2019uqt
;
Frellesvig:2020qot
;
Caron-Huot:2021xqj
;
Caron-Huot:2021iev
, creative
telescoping
vanhove-2021
, or by the Macaulay matrix method.

Suppose that we want to restrict
z_{1}=0
, and that the Pfaffian matrices
P_{i}
are singular there.
The first step in our protocol is to bring the Pfaffian system into
normal form
, meaning (roughly) that the Pfaffian matrix
P_{1}
has a simple pole at
z_{1}=0
, and the remaining matrices
P_{2},\ldots
are finite there.
Normal form is achieved by Moser reduction, an efficient algorithm involving a series of gauge transformations that we review and give references for in
Appendix
B
.

Next we must choose between two cases:
(i) do we seek solutions for the integrals
I
that are holomorphic at
z_{1}=0
, or (ii) do we seek solutions of the form
I^{(n)}(z_{2},\ldots)\log^{n}(z_{1})
that behave logarithmically at the origin of
z_{1}
?
In case (i), one can immediately write a gauge transformation - see (
3.2
) - which yields the restricted Pfaffian system in the variables
z_{2},\ldots

In case (ii), one repeats this gauge transformation procedure several times, once for each power of
\log(z_{1})
.

This Pfaffian-level restriction protocol is efficient and applicable to any Pfaffian system.
While we have only tested it for restrictions to hyperplanes, namely restrictions of the form
z_{1}=f(z_{2},\ldots)
where
f
is a linear function of the remaining variables, we expect that it will be applicable to restrictions onto general hypersurfaces too.

While the whole strategy ultimately only requires gauge transformations applied to Pfaffian systems, we derive and motivate it by studying
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
The derivation is partly conjectural, but we verify it in many examples.

\mathcal{D}
-module-level restriction

In this approach, we assume that a holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-module is given.
One example is the GKZ
\mathcal{D}
-module
1
1

1

Alternative
\mathcal{D}
-modules related to Feynman integrals can also be envisaged, such as the annihilating operators coming from conformal symmetry
Henn:2023tbo
and the Yangian bootstrap, see
Loebbert:2022nfu
and references therein.
.

Traditional
\mathcal{D}
-module restriction algorithms stem from the breakthrough work by T. Oaku
Oaku-1997
.
They rely on Gröbner bases in non-commutative rings, which can become computationally expensive when many variables are involved.
In this work, instead, we extend the Macaulay matrix method from
Chestnov:2022alh
to incorporate restriction of variables.

Our approach contains two steps.
In the first step, we guess a basis of differential operators for the restricted
\mathcal{D}
-module.
This is done systematically via
Algorithm
4.7
.
In the second step, we construct a Macaulay matrix for this basis.
This matrix is polynomial in the
z
variables, and so we can immediately fix desired values for the
z
’s without running into singularities.
The Macaulay matrix induces a linear system of equations, the solution to which is the restricted Pfaffian system (such a linear system can be swiftly solved by rational reconstruction over finite fields
Peraro:2019svx
;
Klappert:2019emp
).
The restriction method based on the Macaulay matrix is summarized in
Algorithm
4.11
.

In comparison to the Pfaffian-level restriction protocol, the
\mathcal{D}
-module level restriction does not require a Pfaffian system as input.
This is an appealing feature, because the original, un-restricted Pfaffian system can often be computationally expensive to obtain.
Furthermore, while the Pfaffian-level restriction method is partly conjectural, the
\mathcal{D}
-module-level restriction protocol is proven in this text to yield the correct restriction module.

The
\mathcal{D}
-module-level protocol works for restriction to general hypersurfaces, in addition to hyperplanes.
As far as we are aware, this is the first instance of such an algorithm.
We illustrate this feature in
Section
5.6
by computing the restriction of the Appell
F_{4}(x,y)
function onto its singular locus
xy((x-y)^{2}-2(x+y)+1)=0
.

The computer algebra software
Risa/Asir
was used extensively in our
\mathcal{D}
-module computations, especially the newly developed package
mt_mm
.

Risa/Asir
can be built from source by cloning the
git
repository

https://github.com/openxm-org/OpenXM
.
Source code for
mt_mm
can be found in the directory
OpenXM/src/asir-contrib/packages/src/mt_mm
.

Symmetry relations

In addition to integration-by-parts relations, some Feynman integrals are also linearly related to one another via
symmetry relations
.
These symmetries stem from reparametrizations of the Feynman
integrand
resulting in the same function after integration.
Although this case is not modeled as a singular limit on a Pfaffian system, we nevertheless observe that symmetry relations can be treated using the Pfaffian-level restriction protocol.
Our test case is the equal-mass limit of the Pfaffian system for the three-mass two-loop sunrise integral - see
Section
5.4
.

Outline

The remaining part of this text is structured as follows.

In
Section
2
, we collect preliminary material on
\mathcal{D}
-modules associated to Euler integrals (a class of integrals which includes Feynman integrals).
This section also defines the notions of Pfaffian systems and restrictions.
To further familiarize oneself with the language of
\mathcal{D}
-modules, we strongly recommend reading the review in
Appendix
A
.

Section
3
introduces the central concept of a
logarithmic integrable connection
, a particular
\mathcal{D}
-module which represents the Pfaffian system we seek to restrict.
The story presented in this section leads us to the three equations (
3.2
), (
52
) and (
3.3
) which together outline the Pfaffian-level restriction protocol.
Some steps in the construction are technical, so in
Section
3.4
we summarize how to apply the method to Feynman integrals in practical terms.
In particular, we there write the holomorphic restriction
Algorithm
3.8
and the logarithmic restriction
Algorithm
3.9
.

Moving on to
Section
4
, we begin our quest to formulate restriction algorithms at the level of
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
We begin by recalling the important notions of weight vectors and
b
-functions in
Section
4.1
, as they pertain to restriction algorithms based on Gröbner bases.
This subsection culminates in
Algorithm
4.7
, which systematically searches for a differential operator basis for the restricted
\mathcal{D}
-module.
Having laid out this groundwork, we are then ready in
Section
4
to formulate the
\mathcal{D}
-module-level restriction algorithm based on the Macaulay matrix - see
Algorithm
4.11
.
Here we heavily rely on earlier results from the manuscript
Chestnov:2022alh
.

Section
5
, making up the largest body of this work, contains a plethora of examples studying restrictions on Feynman integrals and hypergeometric functions.
The goal of each example is always to derive a Pfaffian system which holds in some limit.

We give conclusions and speculate on future developments in
Section
6
.

A long appendix follows the main text.
We have already mentioned
Appendix
A
and
Appendix
B
above.

Appendix
C
describes improvements of algorithms mentioned in the main text.
Finally,
Appendix
D
is a collection of proofs which require technical details on connections and
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
We hope that this layout makes it easier to read the paper.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall the framework of twisted cohomology groups for Feynman integrals and fix basic notation used throughout the paper.
We use terminology on
\mathcal{D}
-modules, such as
regular holonomic
, which is explained in the standard textbooks
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
and
Borel
.
See
(
Chestnov:2022alh
, Appendix B)
for a short introduction to holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules (for a pedagogical presentation which is tailored to Feynman integrals, see also the recent article
Henn:2023tbo
).

Appendix
A
in the present manuscript gives an introduction to tensor products of modules, as they are used extensively throughout this text.

2.1
Twisted cohomology groups as
\mathcal{D}
-modules

We study Euler integrals of the form

\displaystyle f_{\Gamma}(z)=\int_{\Gamma}g(z;x)^{\beta_{0}}\,x_{1}^{-\beta_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{-\beta_{n}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}\quad,\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{n}}{x_{n}}\,,

(2)

which are integrated along a
twisted cycle

\Gamma
, namely an integration contour without boundary along which the branch of the integrand is specified
(
aomoto2011theory
, Chapter 3)
.
The exponents
\beta=(\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
are complex parameters.

g(z;x)
is a Laurent polynomial in
x
with monomial coefficients
z_{i}
:

\displaystyle g(z;x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}\,x^{\alpha_{i}}.

(3)

This is written in multivariate exponent notation, i.e. given an integer vector
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
we set

\displaystyle x^{\alpha_{i}}:=x_{1}^{\alpha_{i,1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{i,n}},

(4)

where
\alpha_{i,j}
stands for the
j
-th component of the vector
\alpha_{i}
.
The monomial exponents
\alpha_{i}
induce an
(n+1)\times N
matrix

\displaystyle A=(a_{1}\ldots a_{N})

(5)

with columns defined by
a_{i}:=(1,\alpha_{i})
, under the assumption that
\texttt{Span}\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}=\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
.
If the variables
z_{i}
are all independent, the Euler integral (
2
) is a solution to a PDE system called the GKZ hypergeometric system
GKZ-1989
.
Generalized versions of Feynman integrals are also solutions to GKZ systems
Nasrollahpoursamami:2016
;
delaCruz:2019skx
.
Indeed, the Lee-Pomeransky representation
Lee:2013hzt
takes the form (
2
), but in this case the
z_{i}
are subject to linear constraints determined by the topology of a Feynman diagram - some of them may be equal to unity, or equal to each other.
Therefore, we allow
z
to vary on an affine subspace
Y=\mathbb{A}^{m}\subset\mathbb{A}^{N}
of dimension
m\leq N
.
The coordinates
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
denote an affine parametrization of
Y
.
Physically,
Y
is the space of kinematic variables, e.g. the masses and momenta associated to a given scattering process.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{m}
(resp.
\mathbb{A}
) be the complex torus (resp. the complex affine line)
2
2

2

The torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}
(resp. the complex affine line
\mathbb{A}
) is equal to
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
(resp.
\mathbb{C}
) as a set.

equipped with the Zariski topology,
We define

\displaystyle X:=\big\{(z,x)\in Y\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\big|\,g(z;x)\neq 0\big\}.

(6)

\pi:X\ \to\ Y
is then the natural projection from the space of kinematic and integration variables
(z,x)
to the space of kinematic variables
z
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
represent the ring of linear differential operators with polynomial coefficients in
z
.
It is generated by
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}
and
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{m}
, over
\mathbb{C}
, with generators subject to the commutation relations
[z_{i},z_{j}]=[\partial_{i},\partial_{j}]=0
,
[\partial_{i},z_{j}]=\delta_{ij}
.
Setting
\mathcal{O}(X):=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m},x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{n}^{\pm 1},\frac{1}{g}]
, we define an action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
on

h=h(z;x)\in\mathcal{O}(X)
by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\bullet h

\displaystyle:=\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{i}}+\beta_{0}\left(\frac{1}{g(z;x)}\frac{\partial g(z;x)}{\partial z_{i}}\right)h.

(7)

The collection of (relative)
k
-forms on
X
is an
\mathcal{O}(X)
-module

\displaystyle\Omega_{X/Y}^{k}:=\bigoplus_{K\subset\{1,\ldots,n\},\,|K|=k}{\mathcal{O}(X)}\,\mathrm{d}x^{K}\,.

(8)

This space is acted on by the covariant derivative in integration variables only:

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}:=\mathrm{d}_{x}+\beta_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}_{x}g(z;x)}{g(z;x)}\wedge-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{i}}{x_{i}}\wedge\,.

(9)

The
n
-th
relative de Rham cohomology group
is then defined as

\displaystyle M_{A}(\beta;Y)

\displaystyle:=\Omega_{X/Y}^{n}\ \Big/\ \mathrm{Im}\Bigl(\nabla_{x}:\,\Omega_{X/Y}^{n-1}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X/Y}^{n}\Bigr)\ .

(10)

The action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
defined by (
7
) induces an action on the quotient
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
, thereby endowing
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
with the structure of a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module.
If
Y=\mathbb{A}^{N}
then
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
equals the GKZ system
Schulze-Walther-2009
,
but we do not assume this unless stated explicitly.

Define the
holonomic rank
of a (left)
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module to be the number of holomorphic solutions
3
3

3

The definition of solutions for a
\mathcal{D}
-module is given in
Appendix
390
.

at a generic point in
Y
.
While this definition is
analytic
, we also give an
algebraic
definition in the next subsection.

We have the following result on
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
:

Theorem 2.1
.

M_{A}(\beta;Y)
is regular holonomic.
If the parameter
\beta
is generic, its holonomic rank is given by the absolute value of the Euler characteristic of a generic fiber,
\big|\chi(\pi^{-1}(z))\big|
.
The latter equals the number of roots of the
likelihood equation
huh2013maximum

\displaystyle{\beta_{0}\over g(z;x)}{\partial g(z;x)\over\partial x_{i}}-\frac{\beta_{i}}{x_{i}}=0\quad,\quad i=1,\dots,n\,,

(11)

when
\beta
be a generic complex vector.

See the proof in
Appendix
D.1
.

Example 2.2
.

Let

\displaystyle I_{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{5}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}^{d}k_{2}}{\bigl[k_{1}^{2}\bigr]^{\nu_{1}}\bigl[k_{2}^{2}\bigr]^{\nu_{2}}\bigl[(p_{1}-k_{1})^{2}\bigr]^{\nu_{3}}\bigl[(p_{12}-k_{12})^{2}\bigr]^{\nu_{4}}\bigl[(-p_{123}+k_{12})^{2}\bigr]^{\nu_{5}}}

(12)

denote the massless two-loop N-box integral family in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions with kinematics

\displaystyle p_{i}^{2}=0\quad,\quad s=2p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\quad,\quad t=2p_{2}\cdot p_{3}\,.

(13)

We used the notation
p_{I}=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}
in the propagators.

Up to a prefactor, the Lee-Pomeransky representation of

I_{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{5}}
can be brought into the form

\displaystyle\int_{(0,\infty)^{5}}g(z;x)^{\epsilon-2}\,x_{1}^{\nu_{1}+\epsilon\delta}\cdots x_{5}^{\nu_{5}+\epsilon\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}

(14)

with

\displaystyle g(z;x)

\displaystyle=x_{1}x_{2}\,+x_{1}x_{4}\,+x_{1}x_{5}\,+x_{2}x_{3}\,+x_{2}x_{4}\,

\displaystyle+x_{2}x_{5}\,+x_{3}x_{4}\,+x_{3}x_{5}\,+x_{1}x_{2}x_{4}\,+zx_{2}x_{3}x_{5}

(15)

and
z=t/s
.
Thus, the space of kinematic variables is given by

\displaystyle Y=\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{10})\in\mathbb{A}^{10}\mid z_{1}=\dots=z_{9}=1\}.

(16)

The two parameters
\epsilon,\delta\ll 1
are analytic regulators in the sense
of
speer1969theory
;
Speer1971
.

The holonomic rank of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
is
3
, as seen counting the number of
solutions to (
11
), but the holonomic rank of the GKZ
system
M_{A}(\beta;\mathbb{A}^{10})
is
9
.

\blacksquare

2.2
Zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-modules and Pfaffian systems

Let
\mathcal{R}_{Y}
denote the ring of differential operators with coefficients in the field of
rational
functions on
Y
(this generalizes
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
, which only contains polynomial coefficient functions).
The field of rational functions on
Y
is denoted by
\mathcal{K}_{Y}
.
When there is no fear of confusion, we write
\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}
) instead of
\mathcal{R}_{Y}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{Y}
).
An
\mathcal{R}
-module naturally arises from a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
{\cal M}
by taking a tensor product
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}{\cal M}
.
Note that given two
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-modules
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, they may fail to be isomorphic as
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-modules even if
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}\mathcal{M}_{2}
are isomorphic as
\mathcal{R}
-modules.
However, as we will see, it is enough to deal with the
\mathcal{R}
-module structure to derive a Pfaffian system.

A zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-module

\mathcal{M}
is, by definition, a left
\mathcal{R}
-module whose dimension over
\mathcal{K}
is finite.
The following proposition holds
(
dojo
, Theorem 6.9.1)
:

Proposition 2.3
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module, then
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}\mathcal{M}
is a zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-module.

When a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
{\cal M}
is holonomic, its
holonomic rank
is
algebraically
defined to be
\dim_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}{\cal M}
.

Theorem
2.12
will show agreement between the analytic and algebraic definitions of the holonomic rank.

Given a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of a zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, there exist
Pfaffian matrices

P_{i}(z)=(p_{ij}^{k})_{j,k=1}^{r}\in\mathcal{K}^{r\times r}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, such that the identities

\partial_{i}e_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p_{ij}^{k}e_{k}

(17)

hold true.
It follows that these matrices are subject to the integrability condition:

\partial_{i}\,P_{j}(z)-\partial_{j}\,P_{i}(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot P_{j}(z)-P_{j}(z)\cdot P_{i}(z)\>.

(18)

Conversely, due to (
17
), a set of matrices
\{P_{1}(z),\dots,P_{m}(z)\}
satisfying the integrability condition (
18
) uniquely determines a left
\mathcal{R}
-module on
\mathcal{M}=\oplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{K}e_{i}
.

For an
r
-dimensional column vector of
functions

I=I(z)
, the system of PDEs

\partial_{i}I(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot I(z)

(19)

is called a
Pfaffian system
.
In the context of Feynman integrals,
I
would correspond to a basis of master integrals.

Example 2.4
.

Let us consider the case of an ODE to give intuition on how an
algebraic
basis
e
, consisting of differential operators with rational function coefficients, is related to an
analytic
basis of functions
I
.
For the moment, we set
m=1
.
Take a univariate differential operator
D=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}a_{i}(z)\partial^{i}
, where
\partial:=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
.

D
then induces an
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}_{D}:=\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{R}D
.
For an unknown function
f=f(z)
, the ODE

\displaystyle D\,f=0

(20)

is equivalent to the Pfaffian system

\displaystyle\partial I=P\cdot I\quad,\quad P=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&1&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
-a_{0}&-a_{1}&\cdots&\cdots&-a_{r-1}\end{array}\right]\quad,\quad I=[f,\partial f,\ldots,\partial^{r-1}f]^{T}\,.

The corresponding algebraic basis is
e=[1,\partial,\ldots,\partial^{r-1}]\subset\mathcal{M}_{D}
.

More generally, consider any
m>0
and a left ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{R}
generated by differential operators
D_{1},\dots,D_{\mu}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Assume that the quotient
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{J}
is zero-dimensional.
Then we can take a finite subset of monomials of partial derivatives
\{e_{i}=\partial^{\alpha_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{r}
such that its equivalence classes in
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{J}
form a basis.
For an unknown function
f(z)
, the system of PDEs

\displaystyle D_{i}\,f=0\ \ \ i=1,\dots,\mu

(25)

is equivalent to the Pfaffian system (
19
) with
I=[e_{1}f,\dots,e_{r}f]^{T}
.
Let us mention that any zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-module is isomorphic to a quotient
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{J}
by a left ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
This is proved along similar lines of
(
leykin-cyclic
, Theorem 1)
4
4

4

The statement in this reference concerns
\mathcal{D}
-modules, but one can write the corresponding proof for
\mathcal{R}
-modules simply by replacing the length of a module by its dimension over
\mathcal{K}
.
.

\blacksquare

The following example gives a free basis for the GKZ system.
It is essential for linking an operator basis to a basis of differential forms, which in turn defines integrands for a basis of Euler integrals.

Example 2.5
.

Let us consider the GKZ
\mathcal{D}
-module
M_{A}(\beta;\mathbb{A}^{N})
, where we
recall that
N
equals to the number of monomials in (
3
).
After choosing a term order in the ring
\mathcal{R}
, a set of standard monomials
{{\tt{Std}}}
is well-defined
(
dojo
, Chapter 6)
.
Each element
e_{i}=\partial^{\alpha_{i}}
of
{{\tt{Std}}}
corresponds to a cohomology class represented by a monomial differential form

\displaystyle\beta_{0}\,(\beta_{0}-1)\dots(\beta_{0}-|\alpha|+1)\>\frac{x^{A\,\alpha}}{g^{|\alpha|}}\>\frac{dx}{x}\>,

(26)

where we set
A\,\alpha:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\cdot a_{i}
.
These cohomology classes provide a free basis of
M_{A}(\beta;\mathbb{A}^{N})
on a non-empty open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{N}
.
A Pfaffian system for
\{e_{i}\}
can e.g. be obtained via a Gröbner basis computation
(
dojo
, Example 6.2.1)
.

\blacksquare

We note that Pfaffian matrices are basis-dependent.
If we choose a different basis
\{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{r}\}
specified by an invertible matrix
G=(g_{i}^{j})_{i,j=1}^{r}
such that
{e}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}g_{i}^{j}\,\tilde{e}_{j}
, then the new Pfaffian matrix is determined by a
gauge transformation
:

G[P_{i}]:=G^{-1}(P_{i}G-\partial_{i}G).

(27)

2.3
Secondary-like equation

This section contains theorems which will be used in
Section
5.4
to extract certain symmetry relations among Feynman integrals.

For a left
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we write
{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
for the set of
\mathcal{R}
-morphisms
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
.
We recall that a left
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is said to be
simple
if any of its
\mathcal{R}
-submodules equal
\mathcal{M}
or
0
.
A proof of the following theorem is given in
Appendix
D.2
.

Theorem 2.6
.

If an
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is simple, then
{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
is a one-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space.

In
Section
5.4
, we use the contraposition of this theorem: if
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})>1
, then the
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
has a submodule
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}
which is neither
\mathcal{M}
nor
0
.
It is also important to note that the converse to
Theorem
2.6
is true for semi-simple
\mathcal{R}
-modules.
An
\mathcal{R}
-module is said to be semi-simple if for any
\mathcal{R}
-submodule
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{M}
, there exists an
\mathcal{R}
-submodule
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.

Theorem 2.7
.

When the exponent
\beta
is generic and real, the
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}M_{A}(\beta;Y)
is a semi-simple
\mathcal{R}
-module.

Theorem 2.8
.

Suppose that an
\mathcal{R}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is semi-simple.
Then
\mathcal{M}
is simple if and only if
{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
is a one-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space.

See the proofs of these theorems in appendices
D.3
,
D.4
respectively.

In practice, it is useful to describe the set
{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
in terms of a differential equation.
Fixing a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of
\mathcal{M}
, any
\mathcal{K}
-linear map
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is represented by an
r\times r
matrix
\Phi
with entries in
\mathcal{K}
.
Starting from (
17
) and using that
\varphi(\partial_{i}e_{j})=\partial_{i}\varphi(e_{j})
, a short calculation shows that
\varphi
is
\mathcal{R}
-linear if and only if the following differential equations hold:

\partial_{i}\Phi=\Phi\cdot P_{i}^{T}-P_{i}^{T}\cdot\Phi\quad,\quad i=1,\dots,m\>,

(28)

which we call the
secondary-like equation
(in analogy with the secondary equation from
matsubaraheo2019algorithm
).

2.4
Restriction of a
\mathcal{D}
-module

Consider an affine subspace
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
, and let
\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}}
denote the coordinate ring of
Y^{\prime}
.
More precisely,
\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}}
is given by the quotient
\mathcal{O}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}_{Y^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{Y^{\prime}}
is the ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
which vanishes on
Y^{\prime}
.
When
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=\cdots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0\}
,
\mathcal{I}_{Y^{\prime}}
is generated by
z_{1},\dots,z_{m^{\prime}}
, in which case
\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m}]/\mathcal{I}_{Y^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}[z_{m^{\prime}+1},\dots,z_{m}]
.
As a note on notation, primed symbols, such as
Y^{\prime}
, will henceforth be associated to restrictions.

For a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
{\cal M}
, we set

\iota^{*}{\cal M}:=\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}{\cal M}.

(29)

We write
\iota:Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
for the natural inclusion.
By equation (
386
) of the appendix,
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
can also be written as

\iota^{*}{\cal M}=\frac{{\cal M}}{z_{1}{\cal M}+\ldots+z_{m^{\prime}}{\cal M}}.

(30)

The left action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
on
{\cal M}
naturally induces that of
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
on
\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}
.
We thus have

Definition 2.9
.

The
restriction
of a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
{\cal M}
to
Y^{\prime}
is defined by the
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module
\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}
.

Recall that the
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
from (
10
) characterizes the vector space structure of Feynman integrals. The following proposition thus links restrictions to our study of Feynman integrals.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
Y^{\prime}
be an affine subspace of
Y
.
Then there is an isomorphism
\iota^{*}M_{A}(\beta;Y)\simeq M_{A}(\beta;Y^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-modules.

Proof.

It follows immediately from the definition of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
and the base change formula of
(
Borel
, Theorem 8.4)

\Box

Definition 2.11
.

The
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-
restriction
of
\mathcal{M}
to
z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0
is defined by
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}{\cal M}
.
If no confusion arises regarding the specification of
\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we simply call
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}{\cal M}
the
rational restriction
of
\mathcal{M}
to
z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0
.

Pfaffian matrices for rational restrictions at the level of
\mathcal{D}
-modules
can be obtained algorithmically as follows.
First, compute a basis
e
of the rational restriction

{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}\iota^{*}{\cal M}
as a vector space over
the rational function field
\mathcal{K}_{Y^{\prime}}
on
Y^{\prime}
.
Second, express
\partial_{i}e
as a linear combination of the basis
e
.
The coefficients of
e
in this linear combination gives the Pfaffian matrix.
For more details, see
Example
4.6
,
Theorem
4.10
,
Theorem
4.14

and
Algorithm
4.11
.

Next we relate restrictions to solution spaces of PDEs.
Elements of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
act on the holomorphic functions of any open set
U\subset Y
by
\partial_{i}\bullet f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{i}}
.
We denote by
{\cal O}^{an}(U)
5
5

5

The superscript
an
stands for analytic; see the discussion in
Remark
A.10
for clarification.

the set of the holomorphic functions on
U
.

{\cal O}^{an}(U)
is equipped with a left
{\cal D}_{Y}
-module structure by this action.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a left ideal of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
.
It is well-known that when
{\cal M}={\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
is holonomic,
i.e. when
\mathcal{I}
is a holonomic ideal,
then the space of holomorphic solutions on
U

\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{Y}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(U))=\left\{f\in{\cal O}^{an}(U)\bigm|\ell\bullet f=0\mbox{ for all $\ell\in\mathcal{I}$}\right\}

(31)

is a finite-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{C}
(see
Appendix
A
).
Let
c
be a point in
Y
.
We denote by
B(c,R)
the open ball with the center
c
and radius
R
.
There exists a number
R_{0}>0
such that

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(B(c,R)))
is
a constant for any
R\leq R_{0}
.
We call it the space of the holomorphic solutions of
{\cal M}
at
z=c
.
The following result is fundamental.

Theorem 2.12
.

See the expositions
(
dojo
, Chapter 6)
,
coutinho
,
(
SST
, Section 5.2)
.

1.

The left
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
is a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.

2.

Let
{\cal M}
be regular holonomic and let
c\in Y^{\prime}
be a point outside of the singular locus of
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
.
Then the holonomic rank of
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
agrees with the dimension
of the space of holomorphic solutions
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(B(c,R_{0})))
when
R_{0}>0
is sufficiently small.
In particular, if
{\cal M}
takes the form
{\cal M}=\mathcal{D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
for some left ideal
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{D}_{Y}
, we have

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}\Bigl({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}\bigl(B(c,R_{0})\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\frac{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}{\mathcal{I}+(z_{1}-c_{1})\mathcal{D}_{Y}+\ldots+(z_{m}-c_{m})\mathcal{D}_{Y}}

(32)

by setting
m^{\prime}=m
. In other words, we set
Y^{\prime}=\{c\}
.

3.

If
c\in Y
does not belong to the singular locus of
{\cal M}
,
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(B(c,R_{0})))
is equal to the holonomic rank of
{\cal M}
.

Let
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}^{k}:=\overbrace{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}\times\cdots\times{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}^{k}
be a free
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.
It is well-known that the restriction
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
can be expressed as

\iota^{*}{\cal M}=\mathcal{D}^{k}_{Y^{\prime}}/\mathcal{J}
for some
k
,
where

\mathcal{J}
is a left
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}^{k}

dojo
;
coutinho
.
The restriction can also be expressed in terms of a Göbner basis as in (
89
).
The holonomic rank of
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
can be determined by computing a Gröbner basis of
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\mathcal{J}
in a free left
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}^{k}
6
6

6

For introductions to Gröbner bases of a submodule in a free module,
see
(
dojo
, Section 3.5)
,
(
adams
, Chapter 3)
, and
Appendix
A
.
The first two references discuss modules over a polynomial ring, but it is easy to
generalize their exposition to left modules over
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
or
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
.

For a given holonomic
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}^{k}/\mathcal{J}
,
there exists a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
such that

{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}/\mathcal{I}
is isomorphic to
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}^{k}/\mathcal{J}
as a
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.
An algorithmic construction of
\mathcal{I}
, relying on the so-called
cyclic vector
, is given in
leykin-cyclic
.
Our
Remark
A.9
gives an easy, albeit inexact, derivation.
The cyclic vector also allows for the construction of an ideal associated to a Pfaffian system.

3
Restriction of a Pfaffian system

In this section, we present our algorithm for the restriction of a generic Pfaffian
system.
In
Section
3.1
, we revise key points in the theory
of integrable connections.
Then in
Section
3.2
and
Section
3.3
, we
show the restriction procedure for a logarithmic
connection and the corresponding solution of the Pfaffian system.
Finally, in
Section
3.4
, we apply the developed theory to
the Pfaffian systems coming from Feynman integrals.

3.1
Integrable connection

The arguments we present in this section are Zariski local, meaning that we always work on a Zariski open subset
Y_{0}
of the total space
Y
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
denote the ring of regular functions on
Y_{0}
.
When
Y_{0}
is a complement of a hypersurface
\{f=0\}
defined by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
is given by
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m},\frac{1}{f}]
.
We set
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}:=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{D}_{Y}
.
The ring structure of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
induces that of
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
.
An
integrable connection

{\cal M}
on
Y_{0}
is a free
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-module of finite rank as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module
7
7

7

In the standard literature, an integrable connection is usually defined as
a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module which is locally free as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module. However, a
locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module is a free module when it is restricted to an
open subset. As we are only interested in the structure of
\mathcal{R}
-modules
in this paper, the global structure of a locally free module does not play
any role. Hence, our definition of an integrable connection does not
involve locally free modules.

.
More concretely,
{\cal M}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}
as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module
for some integer
r
. The action of
\partial_{i}
(for
i=1,\ldots,m
) is
determined by an
r\times r
matrix
P_{i}(z)
with entries in
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
according to the
formula (
17
), where
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
is any
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-free
basis. The integrability condition (
18
) follows straightforwardly for the
matrices
P_{i}(z)
obtained in this way.
Conversely, any set of matrices
\{P_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
with
entries in
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
satisfying the integrability condition
(
18
) gives rise to a connection

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}=\partial_{i}-P_{i}

(33)

by the formula (
17
).
In this representation, the integrability condition translates into the
commutator relation
[\nabla_{i},\nabla_{j}]=0\>
.

Any holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module is an integrable connection on a non-empty open
subset
Y_{0}\>
. For a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
{\cal M}\>
, we define a left

\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-module
{\cal M}|_{Y_{0}}
by
{\cal M}|_{Y_{0}}:=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}{\cal M}
.
As the following proposition shows, studying the
\mathcal{R}
-module structure of a

\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module is the same as restricting it onto an open subset wherein it is an
integrable connection.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
{\cal M}_{1},{\cal M}_{2}
be a pair of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-modules.

{\cal M}_{1}
and
{\cal M}_{2}
are isomorphic as
\mathcal{R}
-modules if and only if there is a non-empty open subset
Y_{0}\subset Y
such that
{\cal M}_{1}|_{Y_{0}}
and
{\cal M}_{2}|_{Y_{0}}
are both integrable connections and isomorphic as
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-modules.

Next, we recall the definition of a logarithmic connection. To simplify the
exposition, let
Y^{\prime}
be an affine hyperplane of
Y
. By an affine linear
change of coordinates, we may assume that
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}\>
. We write

\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})
for the subring of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
generated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime}):=\mathbb{C}\langle z_{1},\dots,z_{m},\,z_{1}\partial_{1}\,,\partial_{2},\dots,\partial_{m}\rangle\>.

(34)

The use of the symbol
\log Y^{\prime}
is justified because (
36
) below has a logarithmic singularity along
Y^{\prime}
.
We further introduce
Y_{0}^{\prime}:=Y_{0}\cap Y^{\prime}
and assume

Y_{0}^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\>
. The relations between the sets
Y\>
,

Y^{\prime}\>
,
Y_{0}\>
, and
Y_{0}^{\prime}
are shown in
Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
The different spaces
Y,Y^{\prime},Y_{0}
and
Y_{0}^{\prime}
wherein the
z
-variables live. The coordinates
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}
and
\{z_{m^{\prime}+1},\ldots,z_{m}\}
parameterize
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
respectively.
Y_{0}
is an open subset in
Y
containing
Y^{\prime}
. The intersection
Y_{0}^{\prime}=Y_{0}\cap Y^{\prime}
is shown as a dark red line.

Next we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}(\log Y^{\prime}):=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})\>.

(35)

The ring structure of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})
induces that of
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}(\log Y^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
logarithmic connection

{\cal M}
on
Y_{0}
along
Y^{\prime}
is a

\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}(\log Y^{\prime})
-module that is free as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module.
More concretely,
{\cal M}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}
as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module
for some integer
r
, and the action of
\partial_{i}
is given by an
r\times r
matrix
P_{i}(z)
with entries in
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
by the formulas

\displaystyle z_{1}\partial_{1}\,e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p_{1j}^{k}(z)\,e_{k}

(36)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\,e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p_{ij}^{k}(z)\,e_{k}\>,\quad i=2,\dots,m

(37)

where
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
is some
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-free basis.

Setting

\displaystyle z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{m})\>,

(38)

the residue matrix of
{\cal M}
along
Y^{\prime}
is given by

P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})=\bigl(p_{1j}^{k}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)_{j,k=1}^{r}\>
.
The residue matrix with respect to a different free basis is obtained by a conjugation of
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\>
.
Therefore, matrix multiplication by
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
from the left is a
well-defined linear operator on

\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}}{\cal M}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
, as it does
not depend on a particular choice of the free basis.
Given a logarithmic connection
{\cal M}
on
Y_{0}
along
Y^{\prime}
we associate to
it a
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{D}_{Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}(\log Y^{\prime})}\mathcal{M}\>
, which is again
denoted by the symbol
{\cal M}
when there is no fear of confusion.
As an
\mathcal{R}
-module,
{\cal M}
corresponds to a Pfaffian system (
19
) with

\displaystyle P_{1}\ \ \text{replaced by}\ \ \frac{1}{z_{1}}P_{1}(z)\,,

(39)

where the factor of
1/z_{1}
comes from (
36
).

An integrable connection
{\cal M}
on
Y_{0}\setminus Y^{\prime}
is said to be

regular
along
Y^{\prime}
if there exists a logarithmic connection

\widetilde{{\cal M}}
on
Y_{0}
along
Y^{\prime}
whose restriction onto
Y_{0}\setminus Y^{\prime}
is
{\cal M}
.
This terminology is compatible with the regular holonomicity of a

\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module in the following sense: if
{\cal M}
is a regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module and
{\cal M}|_{Y_{0}\setminus Y^{\prime}}
is an integrable connection,
then
{\cal M}
is regular. Although the notion of regularity is well-defined,

\widetilde{{\cal M}}
is not uniquely determined by
{\cal M}
.
Let us finally mention
that the regularity of
\mathcal{M}
is equivalent to the corresponding Pfaffian
matrices taking the form (
39
) after a gauge
transformation.

3.2
Restriction of a logarithmic connection in normal form

A matrix is called
resonant
8
8

8

This is different from resonance in GKZ systems.

when it has two distinct eigenvalues with an integral difference.
When
{\cal M}
is a regular connection along
Y^{\prime}
defined on
Y_{0}\setminus Y^{\prime}
, there exists a logarithmic connection
\widetilde{{\cal M}}
along

Y^{\prime}
on
Y_{0}
such that

1.

The restriction of
\widetilde{{\cal M}}
onto
Y_{0}\setminus Y^{\prime}
is
{\cal M}
,

2.

The residue matrix
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
is non-resonant and the only integral eigenvalue, if it exists, is
0
.

We say that such an
\widetilde{{\cal M}}
is in
normal form
.
If
Y^{\prime}=\{z_{1}=0\}
, the normal form Pfaffian matrices associated to

\widetilde{{\cal M}}
will expand as

\displaystyle\frac{P_{1}}{z_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}P_{1,n}(z^{\prime})\,z_{1}^{n}

(40)

\displaystyle P_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{i,n}(z^{\prime})\,z_{1}^{n}\quad,\quad i=2,\ldots,m\,.

(41)

In other words,
P_{1}
has a logarithmic singularity on
Y^{\prime}
, and the other
Pfaffian matrices are finite there (see
Appendix
B
for
more details and proofs).

The existence of a normal form of a logarithmic connection is well-known
(
Deligne
, Proposition 5.4)
.
Working at the level of Pfaffian matrices, normal form can be found via the
standard Moser reduction algorithms of
Barkatou-Jaroschek-Maddah-2017
;
Barkatou-1997
,
which we review in
Appendix
B
.

The next proposition follows from
Definition
3.2
:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
{\cal M}
be a logarithmic connection along
Y^{\prime}
on
Y_{0}
.
Let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
be a free basis of
{\cal M}
and let
P_{i}(z)
be the matrices determined by (
36
) and (
37
).
Let
\mathcal{N}
be the left
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{r}_{Y_{0}}
generated by the row vectors of

\mathbb{1}\,z_{1}\partial_{1}-P_{1}(z)
and

\mathbb{1}\,\partial_{i}-P_{i}(z)

for
i=2,\dots,m
, with
\mathbb{1}
being the identity matrix.
Then the left
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-module
{\cal M}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}^{r}/\mathcal{N}
.

Let
{\cal M}
be a logarithmic connection on
Y_{0}
along
Y^{\prime}
, and suppose non-resonance, so that none of the eigenvalues of the residue matrix
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
is a
positive integer. The restriction
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
as an integrable
connection can be calculated as follows
9
9

9

Recall that
\iota
is the natural inclusion introduced
in
Section
2.4
.

.
We fix a free basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of
{\cal M}
and identify it
with
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}
as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module.
We set

\displaystyle{{\cal M}}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}\,/\,\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\>,

(42)

where
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
denotes an

\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
spanned by the row vectors of

P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\>
.
From the integrability condition
(
18
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})=\bigl[P_{i}(0,z^{\prime}),\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr]\>,\quad\text{for $i=2,\ldots,m$}\>.

(43)

It follows easily that the action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
given by

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\,e_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p_{ij}^{k}(0,z^{\prime})\,e_{k}

(44)

induces an action on the quotient
{{\cal M}}^{\prime}\>
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The restriction
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
is isomorphic to the integrable connection
{\cal M}^{\prime}
as a
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}
-module.

See the proof in
Appendix
D.5
.

As the rank of the residue matrix
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
is constant on

Y_{0}^{\prime}\>
,
{{\cal M}}^{\prime}
is free as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}
-module (with
Y_{0}^{\prime}
being replaced by a smaller Zariski open subset if necessary),
so we may denote by
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
the Pfaffian matrices associated to this integrable connection.
In what follows, we give a procedure for finding the Pfaffian matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
associated to the restriction module
{\cal M}^{\prime}
.

Let
r^{\prime}
be the holonomic rank of
{\cal M}^{\prime}\>
.
For an element
m
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
, we write
[{m}]
for the equivalence class in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
We suppose the existence of a special, free basis
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\}
for

\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
with associated Pfaffian matrices
\widetilde{Q}_{2}(z^{\prime}),\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{m}(z^{\prime})
.
This basis is assumed to have the following special property:
\{f_{r^{\prime}+1},\dots,f_{r}\}
is a basis of

\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
The Pfaffian matrix
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
of
{\cal M}^{\prime}
with respect to the basis

\{[{f}_{1}],\dots,[{f}_{r^{\prime}}]\}
is then given by the first

r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}
block of the matrix
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

In general, our given basis
e_{i}
with associated Pfaffian matrices
P_{2}(0,z^{\prime}),\ldots,P_{m}(0,z^{\prime})
will not automatically yield this block matrix structure.
In other words, we know
e_{i}
and
P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})
but we do not know the special basis
f_{i}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
.
Let us thus proceed to construct the gauge transformation mapping
e_{i}
to the special basis
f_{i}
.

Given our free basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}^{r}
, we can always
find an
r^{\prime}\times r
matrix
B=(b_{ij})
with entries in
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}\>
such that

\{[f_{1}],\dots,[f_{r^{\prime}}]\}
with
f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}b_{ij}e_{j}
forms a free basis of
{\cal M}^{\prime}\>
.
Then, by the definition of
{\cal M}^{\prime}
, there are exactly
r-r^{\prime}

linearly independent rows of the residue matrix
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\>
.
Let
R
be the matrix containing these independent rows.
Writing
R=(r_{ij})_{i=r^{\prime}+1,\ldots,r}^{j=1,\ldots,r}
, then
f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}r_{ij}e_{j}
forms a free basis of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
Aligning the rows of
B
and
R
produces an invertible
r\times r
matrix
M
:

\displaystyle M=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}B}\\
\hline\cr{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}R}\end{array}\right]=\vbox{\hbox{
\parbox[c]{103.86203pt}{\includegraphics[]{figures/full-mat-brace}}
}}\>.

In vector notation, we now have
f=M\cdot e
.

Borrowing notation from (
27
), we thus obtain the Pfaffian matrix
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
associated to
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\}
from the known matrices
P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})
via a gauge transformation by
M^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})=(M^{-1})\bigl[P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr]=\bigl(M\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})+\partial_{i}\,M\bigr)\cdot M^{-1}\>,\quad i=2,\ldots,m.

(47)

The sought-after Pfaffian matrix
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
for the restriction ideal
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, written in the basis
B
, is finally obtained from the first
r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}
block of
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(z^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\bigl(\partial_{i}M+M\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot M^{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}Q_{i}(z^{\prime})}&{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}\star}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}\mathbb{0}}&{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}\star}\end{array}\right].

This formula for obtaining
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
will be essential for calculating restrictions of Pfaffian systems in

Section
5
.
Note that the lower-left block is zero because
\{f_{r^{\prime}+1},\dots,f_{r}\}
is a free basis of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
(see also
Appendix
C.2.1
for a matrix-based derivation of this fact).
This
\mathbb{0}
-block can be used as an intermediate check on practical computations.

Let us conclude this section by stating an assumption used throughout the rest of this paper.
First, recall that on a non-empty Zariski open subset
Y_{0}
of
Y
, the
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-module
M(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
is a regular integrable connection.

Assumption
:
Let
\beta
be generic. The restriction of a normal form of

M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
to
Y^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
M_{A}(\beta;Y^{\prime})

as an
\mathcal{R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.

By
Proposition
3.4
and
Appendix
B
, the restriction of a normal form of

M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
to
Y^{\prime}
can be computed by means of linear algebra.
By this assumption, we can then compute the restriction of
\mathcal{D}
-modules

M_{A}(\beta;Y^{\prime})\simeq\iota^{*}M_{A}(\beta;Y)
from the data of

\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}M_{A}(\beta;Y)\>
.

Remark 3.5
.

Although we are presently unable to prove this assumption, we have verified
in all our examples that the corank of the residue matrix is always
identical to the expected holonomic rank shown in
Theorem
2.1
.
Moreover, we compute the
Q_{i}
matrices in various examples and observe that
they agree with the Pfaffian matrices derived independently using IBP
software.

3.3
Restriction of solutions to a Pfaffian system

We use the same notation as the previous subsection.
In this subsection, we investigate the boundary value problem of Pfaffian systems in normal form.
This can be seen as a singular boundary value problem; see
(
haraoka2002integral
, Section 2)
,
takayama1992propagation
and references therein.

Holomorphic restriction

Let
{\cal M}
be a logarithmic connection on
Y_{0}
along
Y^{\prime}
and let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
be its free basis.
Take
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\}
to be the dual basis to
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
, i.e. each
\phi_{i}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-linear map from
{\cal M}
to
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
satisfying
\phi_{i}(e_{j})=\delta_{ij}
.
By the definition of the action of
\partial_{i}
on
{\cal M}
,
we can write the space of holomorphic solutions of
\mathcal{M}
on an open
set
U
(recall the definition (
31
)) as

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}}({\cal M},\mathcal{O}^{an}(U))=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}I_{i}(z)\,\phi_{i}\biggm|[I_{1}(z),\dots,I_{r}(z)]^{T}\text{ satisfies (\ref{eqn:pfaffian_system}) with (\ref{eqn:connection_matrix_log})}\right\}\>.

(50)

The vector of functions
I(z)=[I_{1}(z),\dots,I_{r}(z)]^{T}
is a solution
10
10

10

Indeed, equation (
50
) implies that for any
m\in\mathcal{M}

we have the homomorphism property

\partial_{i}\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{r}I_{k}(z)\,\phi_{k}(m)\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{r}I_{k}(z)\,\phi_{k}(\partial_{i}m)
.
So if we choose
m=e_{j}\>
, then

\partial_{i}\bigl(\sum_{i=k}^{r}I_{k}(z)\,\phi_{k}(e_{j})\bigr)=\sum_{i=k}^{r}I_{k}(z)\,\phi_{k}(\partial_{i}e_{j}),

resulting in

\partial_{i}I_{j}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{r}I_{k}(z)\,p_{ij}^{k}(0,z^{\prime})\>,

after we use
\phi_{k}(e_{j})=\delta_{kj}

and (
44
).
Thus we see that the functions
I_{j}
indeed satisfy the Pfaffian system
(
19
).

to the Pfaffian system (
19
)
and it will be associated to Feynman master integrals later on.

Set
U^{\prime}:=Y^{\prime}\cap U
.
We derive a realization of
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}}({\cal M}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{an}(U^{\prime}))
similar to (
50
).
Because
\mathcal{M}
is logarithmic, we can expand
P_{1}(z)
as

P_{1}(z)=P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})z_{1}^{-1}+P_{1,0}(z^{\prime})+P_{1,1}(z^{\prime})z_{1}+\ldots

(51)

The column vector

I(z^{\prime}):=I(0,z^{\prime})=[I_{1}(0,z^{\prime}),\dots,I_{r}(0,z^{\prime})]^{T}

then satisfies the following system of PDEs:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}I(z^{\prime})=P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\cdot I(z^{\prime})\>,\quad i=2,\dots,m

(52)

\displaystyle P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\cdot I(z^{\prime})=0\>.

Thus, we have an identification

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}}({\cal M}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{an}(U^{\prime}))=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}I_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\,\phi_{i}\biggm|I(z^{\prime})\text{ is subject to (\ref{eqn:restriction_equation})}\right\}\>.

(53)

We call (
52
) the
holomorphic restriction
of the Pfaffian system
\partial_{i}I(z)=P_{i}\cdot I(z)
, as it pertains to solution vectors
I(z)
which are holomorphic at
z_{1}=0
.
Note that the first condition involving the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
imposes constraints on the solution vector
I(z^{\prime})
. These extra relations lead to a drop in rank in comparison to the unrestricted Pfaffian system.

Solutions to Pfaffian systems (
39
) and solutions of (
52
) are related to each other in the following sense:

Theorem 3.6
.

Suppose that the Pfaffian system (
39
) is
non-resonant. Then there is a one-to-one correspondence between the
solutions
I(z)
of (
39
) being holomorphic
at
(z_{1},z^{\prime})=(0,0)
and the holomorphic solutions
I(z^{\prime})
of
(
52
). The correspondence is given by the
boundary value map
I(z)\mapsto I(0,z^{\prime})
.

See the proof in
Appendix
D.6
.

Logarithmic restriction

So far, we have only focused on limits of Pfaffian systems for which the solution
I
is holomorphic.
However, in physics applications, it is interesting to study solutions that develop logarithmic singularities in the restriction limit
z_{1}\to 0
.
Let us hence define
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(N)}
to be the vector space of functions of the form

\displaystyle f_{0}(z)+f_{1}(z)\log z_{1}+\ldots+f_{N}(z)\log^{N}z_{1}\,,

(54)

where the
f_{i}(z)
denote convergent power series at the origin.
Then we have an identification

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}}({\cal M},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(N)})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}I_{i}(z)\,\phi_{i}\biggm|I_{i}(z)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(N)},\,[I_{1},\ldots,I_{r}]^{T}\text{ satisfies (\ref{eqn:pfaffian_system}) with (\ref{eqn:connection_matrix_log})}\right\}\>.

(55)

We fix a free basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
of
{\cal M}
to identify it with
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}
as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-module.
For any non-negative integer
N
, we set
{{\cal M}}^{\prime}_{N}:=\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}/{\rm Im}\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})^{N+1}
.
As discussed in the construction of
{\cal M}^{\prime}
in
Section
3.2
, it follows that the action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}^{r}
given by (
44
) induces that on the quotient
{{\cal M}}^{\prime}
.

Consider now a vector expression of the form

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(z)+I^{(1)}(z)\log z_{1}+\ldots+I^{(N)}(z)\log^{N}z_{1}.

(56)

We present the following generalization of (
52
), which is a system of PDEs for the vector functions
I^{(i)}(0,z^{\prime})=I^{(i)}(z^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l l}\partial_{i}\,I^{(n)}(z^{\prime})=P_{i,0}(z^{\prime})\cdot I^{(n)}(z^{\prime})\>,&\quad i=2,\dots,m,\ n=0,\dots,N\\[10.0pt]
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})\cdot I^{(n)}(z^{\prime})=(n+1)I^{(n+1)}(z^{\prime})\>,&\quad n=0,\ldots,N\end{array}

with
I^{(N+1)}(z^{\prime})=0
.
We call (
3.3
) the
logarithmic restriction
of (
39
) at order
N
.
We have an identification

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}}({\cal M}^{\prime}_{N},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(N)})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}I^{(i)}(z^{\prime})\,\phi_{i}\biggm|I^{(i)}(z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}^{an},\,I^{(i)}\text{ is subject to (\ref{eq:log_constraint})}\right\}\>.

(59)

Theorem 3.7
.

Suppose that the Pfaffian system (
39
) is non-resonant.
Then there is a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions
I(z)
to
(
39
) of the form
I^{(0)}(z)+I^{(1)}(z)\log z_{1}+\ldots+I^{(N)}(z)\log^{N}z_{1}
and the holomorphic solutions
I^{(n)}(z^{\prime})
of (
3.3
).
More explicitly, the correspondence is given by the boundary value map
I(z)\mapsto I^{(0)}(0,z^{\prime})
.

See the proof in
Appendix
D.7
.

3.4
Relation to Feynman integrals

Let us summarize how the preceding sections relate to the study of Feynman integrals.

Fix a single Feynman integral.
Its associated de Rham cohomology group
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
(see (
10
)) carries the structure of a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module, so the previous results on
\mathcal{D}
-modules and Pfaffian systems also apply to
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
.
In particular, there exist an
r
-dimensional vector of master integrals
I=I(z)
and Pfaffian matrices
P_{i}(z)
such that
\partial_{i}I=P_{i}(z)\cdot I(z)
holds for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Assume that this Pfaffian system has a singularity at
z_{1}=0
.
We recall the notation
z^{\prime}=(0,z_{2},\ldots,z_{m})
.

According to the discussion of
Section
3.2
, there exists a normal form of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
. In practice, this means that we can use Moser reduction to find a set of new Pfaffian matrices
\widetilde{P}_{i}(z)
such (i)
\widetilde{P}_{1}(z)
has a simple pole at
z_{1}=0
, (ii)
\widetilde{P}_{2}(z_{1}),\ldots,\widetilde{P}_{m}(z)
are holomorphic along
\{z_{1}=0\}
, and (iii) the spectrum of the residue matrix
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
is non-resonant (i.e., no difference of eigenvalues is integral) with zero being the unique integral eigenvalue.
By abuse of notation, we keep using the notation
I
for the solution vector in this new basis.

We are interested in a system of
simpler
PDEs which holds in the limit
z_{1}\to 0
.
This problem is split into two cases: (i) the solution vector
I
is finite at
z_{1}=0
, or (ii) the solution vector is
\log
-singular at
z_{1}=0
.

Case (i): Holomorphic restriction

The relevant set of PDEs was given in (
52
).
However, this is written in a redundant fashion because we have not resolved the constraint
11
11

11

This constraint can be thought of as a collection of IBP relations which hold in the limit.

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})=0\,,

(60)

where the limit
z_{1}\to 0
on
I(z_{1},z^{\prime})
is taken at the
integrand
level.
There are
\texttt{Rank[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}
many relations above, so we would like a restricted PDE system which is manifestly of dimension
r^{\prime}=r-\texttt{Rank[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}
.
Assume we know an
r^{\prime}
-dimensional basis
J(z^{\prime})
for the restricted system (in
Section
5.2
and
Appendix
C.2.2
, we show how to construct such a basis from the original,
r
-dimensional one).
This basis fixes the matrix
B
in (
3.2
) according to the relation
J=B\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})
.
Moreover, the matrix
R
in (
3.2
) equals
\texttt{RowReduce[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}
,
where the operation
RowReduce
includes the deletion of zero-rows.
So we now know the matrix
M
from that same formula.
Equation (
3.2
) finally yields the
r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}
-dimensional Pfaffian matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
satisfying

\displaystyle\partial_{i}J=Q_{i}\cdot J\quad,\quad i=2,\ldots,m.

(61)

This restricted Pfaffian system is simpler than the original one in the sense that it has a smaller rank, and it depends on one less variable.
If needed, once
J
has been determined, it is possible to recover
I
by inverting the relation

\displaystyle M\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}B\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})\\
\hline\cr R\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}J\\
\hline\cr 0\\[-5.0pt]
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right]\,,

where the block of
r-r^{\prime}
zeros below
J
on the RHS arises due to (
60
).

Let us summarize these steps in an algorithm.

Algorithm 3.8
.

(Pfaffian-level holomorphic restriction to
z_{1}=0
)

Input
: The
r
-dimensional Pfaffian system
\partial_{i}I(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot I(z),\,i=1,\ldots m
.

Output
: The solution vector
I(0,z^{\prime})
.

1:

Gauge transform
P_{i}\to\widetilde{P}_{i}:=G[P_{i}]
to
normal form
via Moser reduction.

2:

Find the residue matrix
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
and its row-reduced form
R:=\texttt{RowReduce[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}
. Set
r^{\prime}=r-\texttt{Rank[}\,R\,\texttt{]}
.

3:

Choose an
r^{\prime}
-dimensional basis
J
. This yields the
r^{\prime}\times r
-dimensional matrix
B
via
J=B\cdot I(0,z^{\prime})
. If there is no obvious choice for
J
, then
B
can be set to
B=\texttt{NullSpace[}\,R\,\texttt{]}
.

4:

Construct the invertible block matrix

M=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}B\\
\hline\cr R\end{array}\right]
.

5:

For each
i=2,\ldots,m
, compute

\bigl(\partial_{i}M+M\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot M^{-1}

and save the upper-left
r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}
block matrices
Q_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

6:

Solve the Pfaffian system
\partial_{i}J(z^{\prime})=Q_{i}(z^{\prime})\cdot J(z^{\prime})
for
J
.

7:

return

I(0,z^{\prime})=M^{-1}\cdot[J\,|\,0\cdots 0\,]^{T}
.

We visualize this algorithm in
Figure
2
by showing the building blocks of the restricted
Pfaffian system and its solution introduced in
Section
3.2
.
The
red
surface depicts every possible solution
I(0,z_{2})
which
satisfies the constraint
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}(z_{2})\cdot I(0,z_{2})=0
, i.e. the
nullspace of
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
. For a fixed value of
z_{2}
, this nullspace
spans a
1
-dimensional vector space, shown as
red
lines. As

z_{2}
varies, these lines trace out the
red
surface. This
surface thus portrays a basis for the restricted Pfaffian system, as
given by the row-span of the
{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}B}
matrix
in (
3.2
).
For fixed
z_{2}
, the
purple
lines represent the
1
-dimensional
span of the row vectors in
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
. As
z_{2}
varies, we obtain the

purple
surface. This surface hence depicts the
{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}R}

matrix in (
3.2
). Orthogonality of the
purple

surface w.r.t. the
red
surface ensures invertibility of the
M

matrix in (
3.2
).
The
yellow
curve illustrates a
particular
solution vector

I(0,z_{2}):=[I_{1}(0,z_{2}),I_{2}(0,z_{2})]^{T}\>

given some initial condition
I(0,0)
.
Note that this solution is constrained to lie on the
red
surface.

Figure 2:

Algorithm
3.8

exemplified as the
restriction
z_{1}\to 0
of a rank
r=2
Pfaffian system in two
variables
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.

Case (ii): Logarithmic restriction

By the general theory of PDEs
haraoka2020linear
, we expect the solution vector
I(z)
to take the following form in the limit
z_{1}\to 0
:

\displaystyle I^{(\log)}(z)\quad=\quad\sum_{\lambda\,\in\,\texttt{Spec[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}}\hskip-8.5359ptz_{1}^{\lambda}\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{n=0}^{N_{\lambda}}I^{(\lambda,n)}(z^{\prime})\log^{n}(z_{1}).

(68)

The outer sum runs over the unique eigenvalues
\lambda
of the residue matrix
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
, and for each
\lambda
there is a collection of
N_{\lambda}+1
unknown vectors
I^{(\lambda,0)},\ldots,I^{(\lambda,N_{\lambda})}
.
The integer
N_{\lambda}
depends on the structure of the Jordan blocks in the Jordan decomposition of
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
.

For fixed
\lambda,
each
I^{(\lambda,n)}
is subject to the system of PDEs (
3.3
).
This system includes a list of constraints

\displaystyle(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)\cdot I^{(\lambda,n)}=(n+1)I^{(\lambda,n+1)}\quad,\quad n=1,\ldots,N_{\lambda},

(69)

which leads to a drop in rank.
Equation (
3.3
) was specifically written for the eigenvalue
\lambda=0
, but in the general case there is a shift by
-\lambda
as on the LHS.
We observe that the constraints above imply

\displaystyle I^{(\lambda,n)}=(n+1)!\,(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{n+1}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}\quad,\quad n=1,\ldots,N_{\lambda},

(70)

for which reason we only need to compute
I^{(\lambda,0)}
.
Setting
n=N_{\lambda}
above, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{N_{\lambda}+1}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}=0\,,

(71)

giving
\texttt{Rank[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}^{N_{\lambda}+1}\,\texttt{]}
many constraints among the entries of the solution vector
I^{(\lambda,0)}
.

For each
\lambda
, we calculate
I^{(\lambda,0)}
by repeating the procedure from case (i).
The first step is to find an
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}
-dimensional basis
J^{(\lambda)}
, where

r^{\prime}_{\lambda}=r-\texttt{Rank[}\,(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{N_{\lambda}+1}\,\texttt{]}
, so as to fix the matrix
B^{(\lambda)}
in

\displaystyle M^{(\lambda)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}B^{(\lambda)}\\
\hline\cr R^{(\lambda)}\end{array}\right].

It is important that
B^{(\lambda)}
be chosen such that
M^{(\lambda)}
is invertible.
The
R^{(\lambda)}
block encodes the constraints from the residue matrix in (
71
):

\displaystyle R^{(\lambda)}=\texttt{RowReduce[}\,(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{N_{\lambda}+1}\,\texttt{]}\,.

(74)

Because of (
71
), we thus get a block of zeros in

\displaystyle M^{(\lambda)}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}B^{(\lambda)}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}\\
\hline\cr R^{(\lambda)}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}J^{(\lambda)}\\
\hline\cr 0\\[-5.0pt]
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right]\ .

This can be inverted to recover
I^{(\lambda,0)}
once we have computed
J^{(\lambda)}
.
Inserting
M^{(\lambda)}
into (
3.2
), we obtain a collection of
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}\times r^{\prime}_{\lambda}
-dimensional Pfaffian matrices
Q^{(\lambda)}_{i}(z^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}J^{(\lambda)}=Q^{(\lambda)}_{i}\cdot J^{(\lambda)}\quad,\quad i=2,\ldots,m.

(81)

The equations (
70
,
3.4
,
81
) together determine the unknown vectors
I^{(\lambda,n)}
in (
68
), apart from fixing the boundary constants stemming from solutions of the Pfaffian system (
81
).
These constants can e.g. be fixed by comparing with the original integral
I(z)
at special numerical points.

Summarizing all the steps above, we have

Algorithm 3.9
.

(Pfaffian-level logarithmic restriction to
z_{1}=0
)

Input
: The
r
-dimensional Pfaffian system
\partial_{i}I(z)=P_{i}(z)\cdot I(z),\,i=1,\ldots m
.

Output
: The solution vector
I^{(\log)}(z)
.

1:

Gauge transform
P_{i}\to\widetilde{P}_{i}:=G[P_{i}]
to
normal form
via Moser reduction.

2:

Find the residue matrix
\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}
.

3:

for
each unique
\lambda\in\texttt{Spec[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}

do

4:

Compute

R^{(\lambda)}=\texttt{RowReduce[}\,(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{N_{\lambda}+1}\,\texttt{]}

and set
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}=r-\texttt{Rank[}\,R^{(\lambda)}\,\texttt{]}
.

5:

Choose an
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}
-dimensional basis
J^{(\lambda)}
. This fixes an
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}\times r
-dimensional matrix
B^{(\lambda)}
via
J^{(\lambda)}=B^{(\lambda)}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}
. One choice is
B^{(\lambda)}=\texttt{NullSpace[}\,R^{(\lambda)}\,\texttt{]}
.

6:

Construct the invertible block matrix

M^{(\lambda)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}B^{(\lambda)}\\
\hline\cr R^{(\lambda)}\end{array}\right]
.

7:

For each
i=2,\ldots,m
, compute

\bigl(\partial_{i}M^{(\lambda)}+M^{(\lambda)}\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot(M^{(\lambda)})^{-1}

and save the upper-left
r^{\prime}_{\lambda}\times r^{\prime}_{\lambda}
block matrices
Q^{(\lambda)}_{i}(z^{\prime})
.

8:

Solve the Pfaffian system
\partial_{i}J^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})=Q_{i}^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})\cdot J^{(\lambda)}(z^{\prime})
for
J^{(\lambda)}
.

9:

Compute

I^{(\lambda,0)}(z^{\prime})=(M^{(\lambda)})^{-1}\cdot[J^{(\lambda)}\,|\,0\cdots 0\,]^{T}

10:

Compute

I^{(\lambda,n)}(z^{\prime})=(n+1)!\,(\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}-\lambda)^{n+1}\cdot I^{(\lambda,0)}

for each
n=1,\ldots,N_{\lambda}
.

11:

end

for

12:

return
The sum (
68
).

This is a novel approach for computing logarithmic corrections to scattering events.
A pedagogical example is given in
Section
5.2
, where we compute logarithmic corrections to a one-loop box diagram contributing to Bhabha scattering.
In that example we imagine that
z_{1}
denotes a small mass, such as that of the electron.
While the goal of the example is to compute
I^{(\lambda,\bullet)}(z^{\prime})
, the actual, full, solution
I(z)
would read

\displaystyle I(z)\ \ =\sum_{\lambda\,\in\,\texttt{Spec[}\,\widetilde{P}_{1,-1}\,\texttt{]}}\hskip-8.5359ptz_{1}^{\lambda}\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{n=0}^{N_{\lambda}}I^{(\lambda,n)}(z_{1},z^{\prime})\log^{n}(z_{1})\,,

(82)

where
I^{(\lambda,n)}(z_{1},z^{\prime})
is the analytic function satisfying

I^{(\lambda,n)}(0,z^{\prime})=I^{(\lambda,n)}(z^{\prime})
.
Using the recursion relations (
455
,
466
) from the proofs of
Theorem
3.6
and

Theorem
3.7
, presented in
Appendix
D
, it it possible
to reconstruct
I^{(\lambda,n)}(z_{1},z^{\prime})
from

I^{(\lambda,n)}(z^{\prime})
as a power series in
z_{1}
. In other words,
one would thereby obtain terms of the form
z_{1}^{a}\log^{b}(z_{1})
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Explicit calculations of this nature are left for future work.

4
Restrictions as
\mathcal{D}
-modules by the Macaulay matrix method

In this section, we will discuss rational restrictions from the
\mathcal{D}
-module
point of view.
The first subsection gives an algorithm for finding bases of rational restrictions.
The second subsection uses these bases to construct rational restrictions via the Macaulay matrix method.

4.1
Restrictions as
{\cal D}
-modules

Recall the notion of rational restrictions from
Definition
2.11
.
There,
Y
is the
m
-dimensional complex plane and

Y^{\prime}
is
(m-m^{\prime})
-dimensional complex plane in
Y
defined by

z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0
.
The following proposition will be used to prove the correctness of

Algorithm
C.2
later.
A proof is given in
Appendix
D.8
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a regular holonomic ideal of
{\cal D}_{Y}
.
We have the isomorphism of left
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-modules

{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})\simeq{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}{\cal D}_{Y}/(({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})\,.

(83)

The ideal
({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}
is known as the Weyl closure of
\mathcal{I}

(
Tsai2001algorithms
, Definition 5.1)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a left holonomic ideal in
{\cal D}_{Y}
.
The restriction of the left holonomic
{\cal D}_{Y}
-module

{\cal M}={\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
to
Y^{\prime}
is defined by
\iota^{*}{\cal M}={\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+z_{1}{\cal D}_{Y}+\ldots+z_{m^{\prime}}{\cal D}_{Y})
.
We denote the restriction
\iota^{*}{\cal M}
by
{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
When
m=m^{\prime}
, we denote the restriction by
{\cal N}_{0}
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
be a vector and let
w_{i}
denote its
i
-th component.
Define the
(-w,w)
-
order
for
cz^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
, by

\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(cz^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta})=-w\cdot\alpha+w\cdot\beta\,,

(84)

where
z^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
,

\partial^{\beta}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\partial_{i}^{\beta_{i}}

and

\cdot
is the standard inner product.
The order is defined for
\ell\in{\cal D}_{Y}

as the maximum of all monomial
(-w,w)
-orders appearing in
\ell
.
For
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in{\cal D}_{Y}
, we have

\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(\ell\ell^{\prime})=\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(\ell)+\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(\ell^{\prime})
.
Putting
\theta_{i}=z_{i}\partial_{i}
, we note that
\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(\theta_{i})=0
.
Since
w
defines the order among monomials,
it is called the
weight vector
.

Now, let us briefly review an algorithm for
constructing the restriction
{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}
of
{\cal M}

by following the exposition
(
SST
, Section 5.2)
.
Let
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
be a weight vector such that

w_{m^{\prime}+1}=\ldots=w_{m}=0\quad,\quad w_{i}>0\quad,\quad 1\leq i\leq m^{\prime}.

(85)

Let
b(u)
be the
b
-
function
(also called the indicial polynomial)
of the left ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
{\cal D}_{Y}

with respect to
w
.
More precisely,

b(\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}w_{i}z_{i}\partial_{i})

is a monic generator of the principal ideal

\mathbb{C}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}w_{i}z_{i}\partial_{i}\right]\cap{\rm in}_{(-w,w)}\mathcal{I}\,,

(86)

where
{\rm in}_{(-w,w)}(f)
is the sum of the highest
order terms in
f\in{\cal D}_{Y}
such that
the weight for
z_{i}
is
-w_{i}
and
the weight for
\partial_{i}
is
w_{i}

(
SST
, Section 1.1)
12
12

12

We note that several computer algebra systems have commands for computing
b
-functions -
see in particular
(
SST
, p. 194)
for a definition of the
b
-function which is computer algebra friendly and
dojo
for details on computer algebra systems which are useful in this context.

.

Fix a weight vector
w
satisfying (
85
).
Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
be the set of all elements in
{\cal D}_{Y}

such that the
(-w,w)
-order is less than or equal
to
k
.
In other words, we have

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\left\{\sum c_{\alpha\beta}z^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\Bigm|-w\cdot\alpha+w\cdot\beta\leq k\right\}\>,

(87)

which is a free left
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.

Theorem 4.2
.

Oaku-1997
,
(
SST
, Section 5.2)

1.

If
b(u)=0
does not have a non-negative integral root, then the restriction

{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}=0
.

2.

Let
u_{0}
be the maximal non-negative integral root of
b(u)=0

and let

\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}
be a
(-w,w)
-Gröbner basis of
\mathcal{I}

(see
(
SST
, Section 1.1)
).
Then the restriction
{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}
is expressed as

{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}\simeq\frac{\mathcal{F}_{u_{0}}}{\sum_{\{i\in\{1,\ldots,t\}\,|\,\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}g_{i}\leq u_{0}\}}\mathcal{F}_{u_{0}-\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(g_{i})}g_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}\mathcal{F}_{u_{0}+w_{j}}}

(88)

as a left
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module.

The right hand side of (
88
) can also be expressed as

\frac{\sum_{w\cdot\alpha\leq u_{0}}{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}\partial_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}}{\sum_{\{i\in\{1,\ldots,t\}\,|\,\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}g_{i}\leq u_{0}\}}{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}\sum_{w\cdot\beta\leq u_{0}-\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(g_{i})}:\prod_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}\partial_{j}^{\beta_{j}}g_{i}:{|_{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0}}}\,.

(89)

The notation
:\ell:{|_{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0}}
means that we first order
each monomial in
\ell
such that
\partial_{i}
’s come to the right and
z_{j}
’s come
to the left (via
[\partial_{i},z_{j}]=\delta_{ij}
), whereafter we set
z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0
.
For example, we have

:\partial_{1}z_{1}:\big|_{z_{1}=0}=\>\>(z_{1}\partial_{1}+1)\big|_{z_{1}=0}=1.

Note that
u_{0}
may be set equal to a non-negative integer
larger than or equal to the maximal non-negative integral root
of
b(u)=0
.
This procedure will be utilized in the following sections.

Remark 4.3
.

Let us regard
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}
as a vector space over

\mathbb{C}(z_{m^{\prime}+1},\ldots,z_{m})
.
Considering a Gröbner basis in a free
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module for the denominator
of the expression (
89
),
we can take a basis of the vector space
consisting only of monomials in
\partial_{i}
’s.

We proceed by constructing an algorithm for finding the Pfaffian system associated to the restriction
{\cal N}_{Y^{\prime}}
via Gröbner bases for submodules.
This algorithm will lend itself to the Macaulay matrix restriction method presented in
Section
4.2
by providing a standard basis.

In order to simplify our presentation,
we first illustrate our algorithms for
m=2
and
m^{\prime}=1
.
The generalization to more than
2
variables is not difficult, and will be discussed later.
In the rest of this section, we set
x=z_{1},y=z_{2}
and
{\cal D}^{\prime}={\cal D}_{m},{\cal D}={\cal D}_{m-m^{\prime}}
.

We want to restrict a given left ideal
\mathcal{I}={\cal D}^{\prime}\cdot\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{\mu}\}
in

{\cal D}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\langle x,y,\partial_{x},\partial_{y}\rangle

to
x=0
.
The restriction module is defined as

{\cal D}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{I}+x{\cal D}^{\prime}).

As stated in
Theorem
2.12
,
when
{\cal D}^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}
is a holonomic
{\cal D}^{\prime}
-module,
the restriction module is a holonomic
{\cal D}
-module
with

{\cal D}=\mathbb{C}\langle y,\partial_{y}\rangle
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be a left submodule of
{\cal D}^{k+1}
.
The holonomic rank of the left
{\cal D}
-module
{\cal D}^{k+1}/\mathcal{J}
is, by definition,
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}(y)}{\cal R}^{k+1}/({\cal R}\mathcal{J})
,
where
{\cal R}=\mathbb{C}(y)\langle\partial_{y}\rangle
.

Let
\ell\in{\cal D}^{\prime}
and take
k
