{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1\\
\end{array}\right].

Although we explained a general method to find
C
by a syzygy computation in
Section
4.2
, we employ a more efficient method for this case.
As (
108
) is written modulo
L=\underline{x^{2}}+\ldots
, we can eliminate all instances of
x^{p}
for
p\geq 2
via division by
L
.
Consequently, it is sufficient to write
C
as a rational ansatz being linear in
x
:

\displaystyle C=\frac{p_{ij}(a,b,c_{1},c_{2})x+q_{ij}(a,b,c_{1},c_{2},y)}{r(a,b,c_{1},c_{2})x+s(a,b,c_{1},c_{2},y)}\,.

(381)

After this preprocessing, we solve the system of linear equations

\displaystyle\texttt{Numerator[}\,C_{{\tt{Ext}}}-C\cdot M_{{\tt{Ext}}}\,\texttt{]}=0

(382)

for
p_{ij},q_{ij},r
and
s
, which, by (
121
), yields the Pfaffian matrices
P_{x}
and
P_{y}
in the fraction field
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
(the matrices can be downloaded at
dataAndProgramsOfThisPaper
).
As per
oaku-1996
,
P_{x}
and
P_{y}
induce a
{\cal D}
-module on an affine variety.

Let
[g_{1},\,g_{2},\,g_{3}]
denote the linearly independent holomorphic solutions at
(x,y)=(1/4,\,1/4)\in W
.
The standard monomials on the singular locus
W

are given by

[s_{1},\,s_{2},\,s_{3}]=[1,\,\partial_{x},\,\partial_{y}]
.
We then have a
3\times 3
matrix
F_{ij}=(s_{i}\bullet g_{j})
.
On
W
, it holds that
(\partial_{x}\bullet F)\cdot F^{-1}=P_{x}
and
(\partial_{y}\bullet F)\cdot F^{-1}=P_{y}
.
The matrices
P_{x}
and
P_{y}
are Pfaffian matrices on
W
.
Note that these equalities hold on
W
but not outside of
V(L)
,
because the PDE system (
366
) is irreducible
hattori-takayama
.

Let us end our discussion on
F_{4}
with a numerical analysis of the restricted Pfaffian system.
To this end, we parameterize
L=0
by

\displaystyle x=q_{x}(t),\,y=q_{y}(t)\quad\text{with}\quad q_{x}(t)=t,\,q_{y}(t)=t+1\pm 2\sqrt{t}\,.

(383)

Fixing the exponent parameters
(a,\,b,\,c_{1},\,c_{2})=(-2/3,\,1/3,\,1/3,\,1/3)
, we thus obtain an ODE of rank
3
on
V(L)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}g(t)=\left[P_{x}(q_{x}(t),q_{y}(t))\frac{\mathrm{d}q_{x}}{\mathrm{d}t}+P_{y}(q_{x}(t),q_{y}(t))\frac{\mathrm{d}q_{y}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right]\cdot g(t)\,.

(384)

A solution for the
3
-dimensional vector
g(t)
with initial condition
g=[1,\,0,\,0]^{T}

is given in a
python
notebook at
dataAndProgramsOfThisPaper
.

We note that the restriction of
F_{4}
to its singular locus was also studied in
mitsuo1995connection
from a different point of view, utilizing a double cover map.
However, it is not known that this method can be applied to Horn’s functions
H_{1},\ldots,H_{7}
(see
erdelyi
for definitions).
With our general algorithm, we are able to obtain rational restrictions of every system for
H_{i}
onto irreducible components of their singular loci.
The relevant hypersurfaces are given in the following table:

Hypersurface

H_{1}

y^{2}-4xy+2y+1=0

H_{2}

xy-y-1=0

H_{3}

y^{2}-y+x=0

H_{4}

y^{2}-2y-4x+1=0

H_{4}

27y^{2}x-36xy-y+16x^{2}+8x+1=0

H_{6}

y^{2}x-y-1=0

H_{7}

4xy^{2}-y^{2}-2y-1=0

The holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules for each
H_{i}
are of rank
4
, and in all cases the restricted standard basis is
{{\tt{RStd}}}=[1,\partial_{x},\partial_{y}]^{T}
.
In other words, the rational restrictions of
each system is of rank
3
.
See
dataAndProgramsOfThisPaper
for programs for these restrictions.

6
Conclusion

In this work we studied the process of taking limits on Feynman integrals and,
more generally, Euler integrals. Our philosophy has been to study such
integrals from the perspective of the differential equations that they obey; in
particular their Pfaffian PDE systems. The structure of Pfaffian systems, in
turn, is richly encoded in the algebraic language of holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
It is thereby possible to formalize the notion of a limit as a

restriction
of a
\mathcal{D}
-module.
In our work, the motivating example of a
\mathcal{D}
-module has been the GKZ
hypergeometric system, but our results and methods apply directly
to Feynman integrals as well.

A restriction is generally accompanied by a drop in rank. Regarding the
Pfaffian system for some Euler integral, this means that we expect a smaller
and simpler Pfaffian system to survive in the limit. The main result of this
publication is the development of efficient algorithms for computing these
smaller Pfaffian systems. We presented two such restriction algorithms, each
one having unique benefits. The first algorithm, working at the level of
Pfaffian matrices, only requires sequential applications of certain gauge
transformations. This algorithm is able to extract logarithmic corrections in
the limiting variable, a feature that we expect to be especially useful for
particle physics phenomenology. The second algorithm, working at the level of

\mathcal{D}
-modules, computes the restricted Pfaffian system by passing through the

Macaulay matrix
. This algorithm benefits from the use of linear algebra
over finite fields, making it quite efficient, and it does not require the unrestricted Pfaffian system, which can be hard to obtain, as input. While traditional algorithms
have been limited to restriction onto hyperplanes, we have shown that the Macaulay matrix based
method is able to restrict onto hypersurfaces.

In future work, it would be interesting to test our methods on state-of-the-art
examples relevant for current phenomenological applications. While
this paper only attempted to compute logarithmic corrections to the one-loop
case of Bhabha scattering, there is in principle no computational barrier preventing the push to higher
loops and more points. For the sake of increased accuracy, it is then alluring to
also include the mixed power-and-log corrections of the form
z_{1}^{a}\log^{b}(z_{1})
,

a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\>
, as discussed at the end of

Section
3.4
.
On the mathematical side, we
believe that it would be interesting to investigate restriction
ideals

using the Macaulay matrix method. This would also aid in the computation of
symbol alphabets at two loops, as they can be obtained from restrictions of GKZ
systems
Dlapa:2023cvx
.

Appendix A
Modules and tensors in a nutshell

This exposition is independent of the main text and contains a pedagogical
review of tensor products in the context of
\mathcal{D}
-modules, along with several
instructive examples of tensor products also appearing in the text.

Let
R
be a ring with
1
and
{\cal M}
be an abelian group
with the group operation denoted additively by “+”.
Consider a map from
R\times{\cal M}
to
{\cal M}
, whose image
a\,m
for given
a\in R

and
m\in{\cal M}
is denoted multiplicatively
29
29

29

Explicitly, we consider a map
\psi\,:\,R\times{\cal M}\to{\cal M}
and write
its image as multiplication:
\psi(a,m)\equiv a\,m\>
.
We use the same notation for multiplication inside of the ring
R
.

and is referred to as the result of the
action
of
a
on
m
.
Then the abelian group
{\cal M}
forms a
left
R
-module

when it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

For any
a\in R
and
m_{i}\in{\cal M}
, we have

a\,(m_{1}+m_{2})=a\,m_{1}+a\,m_{2}
.

2.

For any
a_{i}\in R
and
m\in{\cal M}
, we have

(a_{1}\,a_{2})\,m=a_{1}\,(a_{2}\,m)\>
, and

(a_{1}+a_{2})\,m=a_{1}\,m+a_{2}\,m\>
.
30
30

30

In other words,

\psi(a_{1}\,a_{2},m)=\psi\bigl(a_{1},\psi(a_{2},m)\bigr)
and

\psi(a_{1}+a_{2},m)=\psi(a_{1},m)+\psi(a_{2},m)\>
.

3.

For any
m\in{\cal M}
, we have
1\,m=m
.

When
R
is a (commutative) field, a left
R
-module is nothing but an

R
-vector space.

A
right
R
-module
is defined analogously
by the following conditions:

(m_{1}+m_{2})\,a=m_{1}\,a+m_{2}\,a\>
,

m\,(a_{1}\,a_{2})=(m\,a_{1})\,a_{2}\>
,

m\,(a_{1}+a_{2})=m\,a_{1}+m\,a_{2}\>
, and

m=m\,1
.

Example A.1
.

Consider the ring of univariate differential operators with polynomial
coefficients
{\cal D}=\mathbb{C}\langle z,\partial_{z}\rangle
.
The quotient set
{\cal M}={\cal D}/(z\,{\cal D})
forms a right
{\cal D}
-module
by defining the right action
[m\,a]
of some
a\in{\cal D}

on
[m]\in{\cal M}
as the product (i.e. composition) of differential
operators in
{\cal D}
and a subsequent reduction to the equivalence class
31
31

31

Here and in the following the square brackets
[\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}]
denote the
equivalence class of some element
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}
under some relation.
Sometimes we will omit these brackets for the sake of brevity if it
does not lead to confusion.

by
z\,{\cal D}\>
.

\blacksquare

Example A.2
.

Let
{\cal D}_{n}
be the Weyl algebra:

{\cal D}_{n}=\mathbb{C}\langle z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}\rangle\>,

that is, the ring of differential operators in
n
variables
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\>
.
The set of length
k
-vectors
{\cal D}_{n}^{k}
is an example of a left

{\cal D}_{n}
-module: The addition is defined by

(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})+(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})=(f_{1}+g_{1},\ldots,f_{k}+g_{k})

and the action of
h\in{\cal D}_{n}
on
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
is given by

(hf_{1},\ldots,hf_{k})\>
.

{\cal D}_{n}^{k}
is called the
free
{\cal D}_{n}
-module
of rank
k
.

\blacksquare

Let us now briefly review the construction of a Gröbner basis in the free module

{\cal D}_{n}^{k}
that we have just introduced above.
We fix a term order
\prec
in
{\cal D}_{n}
.
For a
k
-vector
f=(0,\ldots,0,f_{i},\ldots,f_{k})
with
f_{i}\not=0\>
,
the index
i
is called the
top index
of
f
.
Consider two elements
f
and
g
in
{\cal D}_{n}^{k}\>
. We say that
f
is larger
than
g
in the
position over term
(POT) order of
{\cal D}_{n}^{k}\>
, when
the top index of
f
is smaller than the top index of
g\>
, or when the top
indices of
f
and
g
are both
i
and the corresponding components

f_{i}\succ g_{i}\>
.
The reduction (or the division) algorithm follows from this definition of the
total order. Namely, the
S
-pair
S(f,g)
of
f
and
g
is a
(0,\ldots,0)
vector
when the top indices of
f
and
g
are not the same.
When the top indices are the same and equal to
i
, then the
S
-pair of
f

and
g
is defined by
h_{1}f-h_{2}\,g\>
, where
h_{j}\in{\cal D}_{n}
are chosen
so that
h_{1}f_{i}-h_{2}\,g_{i}
is the
S
-pair of
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
in
{\cal D}_{n}
.

Example A.3
.

Consider the free module
{\cal D}_{2}^{2}
in two variables
z_{1}
and
z_{2}\>
.
When
f=(\partial_{1},z_{1})
and
g=(0,z_{1})\>
,
then
S(f,g)=(0,0)
as the top indices of
f
and
g
are not the same.
If
f=(z_{1}\,\partial_{1},z_{1})
and
g=(z_{2}\,\partial_{1},z_{1})
,
then the
S
-pair becomes
S(f,g)=z_{2}\,f-z_{1}\,g=(0,z_{1}z_{2}-z_{1}^{2})\>
.
For further examples and details we refer the interested reader to

adams
and
(
dojo
, Sections 6.6-7)
.

\blacksquare

Definition A.4
.

For a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module
{\cal M}
and a right
{\cal D}_{n}
-module
P
, let us define
the tensor product
P\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
as follows. We denote by
p\otimes m
the element of the direct product
(p,m)
, where
p\in P
and
m\in{\cal M}
. Then
P\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
is the set of a finite formal sum of elements of this
form modulo the equivalence relation
\sim
given by:

1.

p_{1}\otimes m+p_{2}\otimes m\sim(p_{1}+p_{2})\otimes m
,

m\in{\cal M}
,
p_{i}\in P
(linearity in the left slot).

2.

p\otimes m_{1}+p\otimes m_{2}\sim p\otimes(m_{1}+m_{2})
,

p\in P
,
m_{i}\in{\cal M}
(linearity in the right slot).

3.

pf\otimes m\sim p\otimes fm

for
f\in{\cal D}_{n}
,
m\in{\cal M}
, and
p\in P\>
.
32
32

32

Hence the subscript
{\cal D}_{n}
in
\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}
means that any element
f\in{\cal D}_{n}

can be carried across the
\otimes
sign.

In other words, if we denote by
F(P\otimes{\cal M})
the set of formal finite sums of
p\otimes m
,
then the tensor product is just the factor

P\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}:=F(P\otimes{\cal M})/\sim\>
.

Remark A.5
.

When the left entry
P
is also a left
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-module,
the tensor product
P\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
can be turned into a left

{\cal D}_{n-1}
-module by specifying the action of
g\in{\cal D}_{n-1}
on
p\otimes m
as
g\,(p\otimes m)=(g\,p)\otimes m\>
.

Example A.6
.

Let
{\cal M}
be a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module.
In order to illustrate the various concepts introduced so far,
here we show a proof of the following well-known isomorphism between two left
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-modules written as quotients:

{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}\simeq{\cal M}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal M})

(385)

We start with showing that the right
{\cal D}_{n}
-module
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})

(see
Example
A.1
), while being also a left
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-module,
is in fact
not
a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module.
Indeed, suppose that it is a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module.
We denote by
[\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}]
the equivalence class of some element
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}

in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})
.
On one hand,
[z_{n}]=[0]
in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\>
, so that the action
of the derivative
\partial_{n}
should also be
33
33

33

Note that the condition
a([0]+[0])=a([0])=a[0]+a[0]
implies

a[0]=[0]
for any
a\in{\cal D}_{n}
.

\partial_{n}[z_{n}]=[0]
in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})
.
On the other hand,
\partial_{n}[z_{n}]=[z_{n}\partial_{n}+1]
and

[z_{n}\partial_{n}]=[0]
in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\>
,
leading to
\partial_{n}[z_{n}]=[1]\neq[0]
in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\>
,
which is a contradiction.
The following technical observation will become useful shortly:
any non-zero element of
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

can be expressed as
[1]\otimes m
for some
m\not\in z_{n}\,{\cal M}\>
.
Indeed, in a single product any factor
f\in{\cal D}_{n}
can be moved from the
left to the right slot:

[f]\otimes m=[1]\otimes fm\>
.
After performing this operation termwise,
any finite sum can be united into a single product:

[1]\otimes m_{1}+[1]\otimes m_{2}=[1]\otimes(m_{1}+m_{2})\>

according to
Definition
A.4
.
For two non-zero elements
[1]\otimes m_{1}
and
[1]\otimes m_{2}\>
, where

m_{i}\not\in z_{n}\,{\cal M}\>
,
it can be shown that
the condition
[1]\otimes m_{1}\not=[1]\otimes m_{2}
is equivalent to

m_{1}\not=m_{2}
in
{\cal M}
.
We also note that
[0]\otimes m
and
[f]\otimes 0
are

0
by definition.

We define a
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-morphism
\varphi

from
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

to
{\cal M}/(z_{n}{\cal M})
by

\varphi\bigl([f]\otimes m\bigr):=[fm]

and extend this definition on finite sums

\varphi\bigl([f_{1}]\otimes m_{1}+[f_{2}]\otimes m_{2}\bigr)=[f_{1}m_{1}]+[f_{2}m_{2}]\>
by linearity.
Let us now prove that
\varphi
is indeed an isomorphism.

\varphi
is well-defined:

We prove that if
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
,

t_{i}\in{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
,
then
\varphi(t_{1})=\varphi[t_{2}]
.
From the definition of
\varphi
it follows that if
t\sim 0\otimes 0
, then
\varphi(t)=[0]
.
Any
0
in
{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

can be expressed as
[z_{n}]\otimes m
,
m\in{\cal M}

after a simplification by “+”.
Note that
\varphi
satisfies
\varphi(t_{1}+t_{2})=\varphi(t_{1})+\varphi(t_{2})

for any
t_{i}\in{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,D_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
.
Since

\varphi\bigl([z_{n}]\otimes m\bigr)=[z_{n}m]
,
z_{n}m\in z_{n}{\cal M}
,
then
[0]\otimes m
is sent to
[0]
by
\varphi
.

\varphi
is
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-morphism:

Let
c_{i}\in{\cal D}_{n-1}
,
f_{i}\in{\cal D}_{n}
, and
m_{i}\in{\cal M}
.

\displaystyle\varphi\bigl(c_{1}\,([f_{1}]\otimes m_{1})+c_{2}\,([f_{2}]\otimes m_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi\bigl([c_{1}\,f_{1}]\otimes m_{1}+[c_{2}\,f_{2}]\otimes m_{2}\bigr)

(
Remark
A.5
)

\displaystyle=[c_{1}\,f_{1}\,m_{1}]+[c_{2}\,f_{2}\,m_{2}]

(definition of
\varphi
)

\displaystyle=c_{1}\,[f_{1}\,m_{1}]+c_{2}\,[f_{2}\,m_{2}]

(action of
c_{i}
on
{\cal M}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal M})
)

\displaystyle=c_{1}\,\varphi\bigl([f_{1}]\otimes m_{1}\bigr)+c_{2}\,\varphi\bigl([f_{2}]\otimes m_{2}\bigr)\qquad

(definition of
\varphi
)

\varphi
is surjective:

Clearly the image
\varphi\bigl([1]\otimes{\cal M}\bigr)
covers the whole

{\cal M}\big/z_{n}\,{\cal M}
.

\varphi
is injective:

Due to the linearity of
\varphi
, it is enough to prove that if

\varphi\bigl(t\bigr)=0

for some
t\in{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

,
then necessarily
t=0
.
Suppose that
t\not=0
.
Then there exists
m\in{\cal M}
such that
t\sim[1]\otimes m
.
Since
\varphi\bigl([1]\otimes m\bigr)=[m]=0
, we have

m\in z_{n}\,{\cal M}
.
Writing
m=z_{n}\,m^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime}\in{\cal M}
, we have

[1]\otimes(z_{n}\,m^{\prime})\sim[z_{n}]\otimes m^{\prime}\sim 0\otimes m^{\prime}
,
which means that
t=0
.

Thus we constructed an explicit isomorphism
\varphi
between

{\cal D}_{n}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal D}_{n})\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
and
{\cal M}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal M})
and proved
the identity (
385
).

\blacksquare

Example A.7
.

Another example of tensor product is:

{\cal N}=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]\big/\langle z_{n}\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]}{\cal M}\>.

For
1\leq i\leq n-1

we define the action of
\partial_{i}
on the product

[f]\otimes m\>
as

\partial_{i}([f]\otimes m):=[\partial_{i}f]\otimes m+[f]\otimes(\partial_{i}m)\>
,
where
[f]\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]\big/\langle z_{n}\rangle
and

m\in{\cal M}\>
.
Multiplication by
h\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1}]
is defined by

h\,([f]\otimes m):=[h\,f]\otimes m\>
(similar to
Remark
A.5
).
Then we can prove that
{\cal N}
is a left
{\cal D}_{n-1}
-module

{\cal N}\simeq{\cal M}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal M})\>.

(386)

Indeed, the isomorphism
\varphi
from
{\cal N}
to
{\cal M}\big/(z_{n}\,{\cal M})
is given by

\varphi\bigl([f]\otimes m\bigr):=[f\,m]\>
.
Note that for
[f]=[z_{n}\,h]\sim[0]
we have

\varphi\bigl([z_{n}\,h]\otimes m\bigr)=[z_{n}\,h\,m]\sim[0]\>
, which makes the map
\varphi
is well-defined.
It is left to the reader to prove that
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

\blacksquare

Example A.8
.

Our final example of tensor product is

\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{n}
,
where
\mathbb{C}[z]=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
and
f
is a polynomial in
z\>
.
Since the left entry
\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},1/f]
can be viewed as
a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module, we can regard the tensor product

\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{n}
as a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module by

\partial_{i}(g\otimes\ell)=(\partial_{i}g)\otimes\ell+g\otimes(\partial_{i}\ell)\>
,
for
g\in\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]
and
\ell\in{\cal D}_{n}\>
.
It is an exercise for the reader to describe the action of a general element
of
{\cal D}_{n}
explicitly.
Furthermore, we can introduce the ring structure on

\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{n}
by defining the product “
\cdot
”
between two elements as

(g_{1}\otimes\ell_{1})\cdot(g_{2}\otimes\ell_{2})=g_{1}\otimes\ell_{1}\,(g_{2}\otimes\ell_{2})\>
, where

g_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]
and
\ell_{i}\in{\cal D}_{n}\>
.
Roughly speaking,
\mathbb{C}[z][1/f]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{n}
is

\mathbb{C}\langle z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},1/f,\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}\rangle\>
.

\blacksquare

In
Proposition
2.3
,
we construct a zero-dimensional
\mathcal{R}
-module from a holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module by a tensor product.
The zero-dimensional one can be expressed as a quotient by a left ideal
as the right hand side of (
387
) below.
Let us sketch a proof of the isomorphism.
Take a left ideal
\mathcal{I}
in the Weyl algebra
{\cal D}_{n}\>
, and let
{\cal M}:={\cal D}_{n}\big/\mathcal{I}\>
be a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module.
Let
{\cal R}_{n}=\mathbb{C}(z)\langle\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}\rangle

be the ring of differential operators with rational function coefficients,
where
\mathbb{C}(z):=\mathbb{C}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is the field of rational functions.
The left
{\cal R}_{n}
-modules
{\cal R}_{n}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}
and

{\cal R}_{n}\big/({\cal R}_{n}\,\mathcal{I})
are isomorphic as left
{\cal R}_{n}
-modules.
Here the left action of
h\ni{\cal R}_{n}
on
f\otimes g\in{\cal R}_{n}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

is defined by
h\,f\otimes g\>
.
The isomorphism is given by

{\cal R}_{n}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}\ni f\otimes g\mapsto fg\in{\cal R}_{n}\big/({\cal R}_{n}\,\mathcal{I})\>,

(387)

and we can easily check that it is an isomorphism of left
{\cal R}_{n}
-modules
by noting that
{\cal R}_{n}
is an associative algebra.
Note that

f_{1}f_{2}\otimes g=f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\,g
in
{\cal R}_{n}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal M}

when
f_{2}\in{\cal D}_{n}\>
.
We can show analogously that
{\cal R}_{n}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{n}}{\cal D}_{n}^{m}\big/\mathcal{J}
is
isomorphic to
{\cal R}_{n}^{m}\big/({\cal R}_{n}\,\mathcal{J})
as left
{\cal R}_{n}
-modules, where

\mathcal{J}
is some left submodule of
{\cal D}_{n}\>
.

Remark A.9
.

We referred to the algorithm of constructing a
cyclic vector
in
Section
2
.
It is a way to construct an ideal given a Pfaffian system.

In order to apply the cyclic vector to
{\cal R}_{n}^{m}\big/({\cal R}_{n}\,\mathcal{J})
, we first compute the Weyl closure of
\mathcal{J}
, and then apply the algorithm of
leykin-cyclic
to the closure.
This procedure is exact, but it needs huge computational resources.
We present an easy but inexact method. For simplicity, we illustrate the case of
m=2
,
n=1
(
\partial=\partial_{1}
,
z=z_{1}
), and
{\cal R}_{1}\mathcal{J}
is generated by

(\partial,0)-(a,b),\quad(0,\partial)-(c,d),\quad a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}(z).

These generators induce a system of differential equations for two unknown functions
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
:

\partial\bullet u^{1}-au^{1}-bu^{2}=0,\quad\partial\bullet u^{2}-cu^{1}-du^{2}=0.

(388)

Differentiating both equations w.r.t.
z
, we obtain two more identities:

u^{1}_{zz}-a_{z}u^{1}-au^{1}_{z}-b_{z}u^{2}-bu^{2}_{z}=0,u^{2}_{zz}-c_{z}u^{1}-cu^{1}_{z}-d_{z}u^{2}-du^{2}_{z}=0

(389)

where
{u}_{z}
and
{u}_{zz}
denote the first and second derivatives w.r.t.
z
.
We regard
u^{1},u^{2},u^{1}_{z},u^{2}_{z},u^{1}_{zz}
and
u^{2}_{zz}
as independent variables in the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(z)[u^{1},u^{2},u^{1}_{z},u^{2}_{z},u^{1}_{zz},u^{2}_{zz}]

and eliminate
u^{2},u^{2}_{z},u^{2}_{zz}
from (
388
) and (
389
).
Then we obtain an ODE
u^{1}_{zz}+c_{1}(z)u^{1}_{z}+c_{2}(z)u^{1}=0
for the function
u^{1}
.
In some cases, the left ideal
\mathcal{I}=\langle\partial^{2}+c_{1}\partial+c_{2}\rangle

in
{\cal R}_{1}
gives an isomorphism of

{\cal R}_{1}^{2}\big/({\cal R}_{n}\,\mathcal{J})
and
{\cal R}_{1}/\mathcal{I}
.

Remark A.10
.

As a historical concluding remark, let us rephrase the notion of a
solution
to a system of differential equations in the language of homological algebra, which is a starting point of the theory of
{\cal D}
-modules.
Let us denote by
{\cal O}^{an}(U)
the set of holomorphic functions
34
34

34

The superscript “an” stands for “analytic”. On the other hand,
{\cal O}^{alg}(U)
is the set
of rational functions that do not have poles on
U
.
They are called regular functions on
U
.
We omit the superscript “alg”, that stands for “algebraic”, in this paper.

on an open set
U\>
, and by

\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{n}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(U))\>

(390)

the set of left
{\cal D}_{n}
-morphisms from the left
{\cal D}_{n}
-module
{\cal M}
to

{\cal O}^{an}(U)\>
.
The correspondence between a homomorphism

\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{n}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(U))
and a solution
f
is given by

f=\varphi\bigl([1]\bigr)\>
.
In fact, take
{\cal M}:={\cal D}_{n}\big/\mathcal{I}
, and let
\ell
be
an element of the left ideal
\mathcal{I}\>
, so that
[\ell]\sim[0]
in
{\cal M}\>
.
Since
\varphi
is a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-morphism, we have:

0=\varphi\bigl([0]\bigr)=\varphi\bigl([\ell]\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(\ell\,[1]\bigr)=\ell\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\varphi\bigl([1]\bigr)\>,

(391)

which means that
\varphi\bigl([1]\bigr)\in{\cal O}^{an}(U)
is a solution to
the differential equation
\ell\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}f=0\>
.
Conversely, let
f
be a solution of
\mathcal{I}\>
, meaning that for any
\ell\in\mathcal{I}
it satisfies
\ell\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}f=0\>
.
Then for and other element
m\in{\cal D}_{n}\>
, the mapping

m\mapsto m\bullet f\in{\cal O}^{an}(U)
defines a left
{\cal D}_{n}
-morphism,
i.e. an element of
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{n}}({\cal M},{\cal O}^{an}(U))\>
.
The derived functor of
\mathrm{Hom}
gives cohomological solution spaces
in the theory of
{\cal D}
-modules
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
.

Appendix B
Normal form by Moser reduction

In
Section
3.2
, it was emphasised that a Pfaffian system must be in
normal form
before the restriction protocol can proceed.
A given set of Pfaffian matrices
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}
is in normal form w.r.t.
z_{1}
when
P_{1}
satisfies two conditions:

1.

Logarithmic
:
P_{1}
has a simple pole at
z_{1}=0
.

2.

Non-resonant
: The eigenvalues of the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
have no integral difference, and
0
is the unique integer eigenvalue.

As explained later in this section, the non-resonance condition additionally implies that
P_{2},\ldots,P_{m}
are finite at
z_{1}=0
.
This is important, because in the restriction protocol of
Section
3.2
we use the matrices
P_{2}(0,z^{\prime}),\ldots,P_{m}(0,z^{\prime})
, where
z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{m})
.

Normal form can be obtained by following the Moser reduction scheme
Moser
, which was later made algorithmic by Barkatou
Barkatou1995rational
(see also
Gerard-Ramis-1983
).
For CAS implementations, see e.g.
ISOLDE

barkatou2013isolde
and
Fuchsia

Gituliar:2017vzm
.
For the reader’s convenience, in this section we review Moser reduction, largely following
Barkatou-Jaroschek-Maddah-2017
.

B.1
Logarithmic
P_{1}

Let
\mathbb{1}_{r}
denote the
r
-dimensional identity matrix. The following theorem is a result of
Moser
;
Barkatou1995rational
.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
P(z)
be an
r\times r
matrix, depending on a single variable
z
, with entries in the field of rational functions
\mathbb{C}(z)
.
Assume that a system of ODEs

\frac{dI}{dz}=P(z)\cdot I

(392)

is regular singular at
z=0
.
After a finite sequence of gauge transforms
P\to G[P]=G^{-1}(P\cdot G-\partial_{z}G)
with
G
of the form

\displaystyle G=\text{a constant matrix,}\quad\text{or}\quad G=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}z\mathbb{1}_{r^{\prime}}&\mathbb{0}\\
\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{1}_{r-r^{\prime}}\end{array}\right]\,,\ \text{for}\ r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\,,

the ODEs (
392
) can be transformed into a system with only simple poles at
z=0
.
Such a sequence of gauge transforms can be found algorithmically.

Remark B.2
.

The gauge transformation matrices can be constructed without algebraic extensions.
In other words, if the entries of
P(z)
belong to
K(z)
for some subfield
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, then
G
can be taken to have coefficients in
K
.
This is useful when the system (
392
) contains additional parameters independent of
z
.

The theorem above pertains to ODEs, but Pfaffian systems are PDEs in general.
Fortunately, we can still apply the theorem to the PDE case by treating one variable at a time.
Consider a Pfaffian system in
m
variables:
\partial_{i}I=P_{i}(z)\cdot I\>

, for
i=1,\ldots,m\>
.
Assume that the
P_{i}
have higher-order poles along
z_{1}=0\>
.
Treat

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}I}{\mathrm{d}z_{1}}=P_{1}(z_{1},z^{\prime})\cdot I

(395)

as an ODE in
z_{1}
with parameters
z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{m})\>
.
By
Theorem
B.1
and
Remark
B.2
, we can find a gauge
transformation matrix
G\>
such that
G[P_{i}]
only has
a simple pole along
z_{1}=0\>
. We may thus assume that the Pfaffian matrices
take the form:

\displaystyle P_{1}(z)=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}P_{1,n}(z^{\prime})\,z_{1}^{n}\,.

(396)

This solves the problem of finding a logarithmic set of Pfaffian matrices.

B.2
Non-resonant
P_{1,-1}

Next we show how to cure the resonant spectrum of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
.
The first thing to note is that the eigenvalues of
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
are independent of
z^{\prime}

(
haraoka2020linear
, Theorem 12.1)
.
Therefore, if
P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime})
is non-resonant at a point, then it is non-resonant everywhere.
Fix a generic value
z^{\prime}=a^{\prime}
, and set
P:=P_{1,-1}(a^{\prime})
.
Suppose that
P
is in Jordan normal form, namely in block-diagonal form with Jordan cells
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}
.
Let
r_{i}
denote the size of each
J_{i}
.
Subdivide the matrix
P_{1}(z)
into cells as

P_{1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}(P_{1})_{11}&(P_{1})_{12}&\dots&(P_{1})_{1k}\\
\hline\cr(P_{1})_{21}&(P_{1})_{22}&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\ddots&\\
\hline\cr(P_{1})_{k1}&\dots&&(P_{1})_{kk}\end{array}\right]

(397)

and construct the gauge transformation matrix

\displaystyle G=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}z_{1}\mathbb{1}_{r_{1}}&\mathbb{0}&\dots&\mathbb{0}\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{1}_{r_{2}}&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\ddots&\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&\dots&&\mathbb{1}_{r_{k}}\end{array}\right]\,.

By assumption,
P_{1}
has a simple pole along
z_{1}=0
, and the same will hold true for
G[P_{1}]
.
A short calculation shows that the residue matrix of
G[P_{1}]
evaluated at
z^{\prime}=a^{\prime}
is of the form

G[P]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c}J_{1}-\mathbb{1}_{r_{1}}&(P_{1,0})_{12}&(P_{1,0})_{13}&\cdots&(P_{1,0})_{1k}\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&J_{2}&\mathbb{0}&\dots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&\dots&&&J_{k}\end{array}\right]\,.

(402)

We have thereby shifted the eigenvalue corresponding to the Jordan block
J_{1}
by
1
.
Repeating this procedure sufficiently many times for each
J_{i}
, we will render
P
non-resonant at
z^{\prime}=a^{\prime}
, and therefore non-resonant everywhere.

Let us now show that non-resonance implies finiteness of
P_{2},\ldots,P_{m}
.
The following lemma is well-known.

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose that
A,B\in M_{r}(\mathbb{C})
are
r\times r
matrices with their entries in
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
\lambda(A),\lambda(B)
denote the sets of eigenvalues for
A,B
.
Consider the linear map

T_{A,B}:M_{r}(\mathbb{C})\ni X\mapsto A\cdot X-X\cdot B\in M_{r}(\mathbb{C})\,.

(403)

Then the set of eigenvalues of
T_{A,B}
equals the set of eigenvalue differences
\lambda(A)-\lambda(B)
.

It follows from the integrability condition (
18
) and (
396
) that

\displaystyle\left[P_{1,-1}(z^{\prime}),\,P_{i,n}(z^{\prime})\right]=n\,P_{i,n}(z^{\prime})\quad,\quad i=2,\ldots m\quad,\quad n<0\,.

(404)

We can rewrite this in terms of the map
T_{A,B}
as

\displaystyle T_{P_{1,-1},\,P_{1,-1}}(P_{i,n})=n\,P_{i,n}(z^{\prime})\,,

(405)

meaning that
P_{i,n}
is an eigenvector
of
T_{P_{1,-1},\,P_{1,-1}}
with eigenvalue
n
.
On the other hand,
Lemma
B.3
shows that any eigenvalue of
T_{P_{1,-1},\,P_{1,-1}}
must be of the form
\lambda_{a}-\lambda_{b}
, where
\lambda_{a,b}
are eigenvalues of
P_{1,-1}
.
If
P_{1,-1}
is non-resonant, we cannot have that
\lambda_{a}-\lambda_{b}=n
.
Consequently,
P_{2,n}(z^{\prime})=0
for
n<0
.

Summarizing the previous two sections, we have a

Theorem B.4
.

The algorithm for transforming a Pfaffian system into normal form w.r.t.
z_{1}
is as follows:

1.

Apply the Moser reduction scheme from
Theorem
B.1
over the field
\mathbb{C}(z^{\prime})
to ensure that
P_{1}
has a simple pole at
z_{1}=0
.

2.

Apply gauge transformations of the form (
402
) until
P_{1,-1}
becomes non-resonant.

This theorem can be sequentially applied to several variables, in case one seeks to restrict more than one variable.

B.3
Jordan normal form without field extensions

We have already noted that Moser reduction can be performed using only linear algebra without field extensions.
Step
1
of
Theorem
B.4
is therefore efficient to implement.
Step
2
, on the other hand, apparently requires one to solve polynomials equations, as this is the standard way of computing Jordan normal forms.
Here we argue that algebraic extensions can
also
be avoided in step
2
, wherefore
Theorem
B.4
does not require them at all.
Fix a subfield
K\subset\mathbb{C}
.
The purpose of this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma B.5
.

There exists an invertible matrix
Q\in K^{r\times r}
such that
Q^{-1}PQ
is block diagonalized as

\displaystyle Q^{-1}PQ=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}B_{1}&\mathbb{0}&\dots&\mathbb{0}\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&B_{2}&&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\ddots&\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}&\dots&&B_{k}\end{array}\right]

where each
B_{i}
is non-resonant.
We write
\texttt{Spec[}\,B_{i}\,\texttt{]}
for the set of eigenvalues of the block
B_{i}
.
Then, for any pair of indices
i,j
, either

(\texttt{Spec[}\,B_{i}\,\texttt{]}+\mathbb{Z})\cap\texttt{Spec[}\,B_{j}\,\texttt{]}=\varnothing

or there exists a unique integer
m
such that

\texttt{Spec[}\,B_{i}\,\texttt{]}+m=\texttt{Spec[}\,B_{j}\,\texttt{]}
.

Once this lemma is proved, we can conclude that the gauge transformations involving Jordan normal forms from step
2
of
Theorem
B.4
can be algorithmically chosen from
GL_{r}(K[z_{1}^{\pm}])
, which is free of field extensions.
Indeed, each Jordan block
J_{i}
of
P
is replaced by the block
B_{i}
.
Let us begin with a simple lemma.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
f(t)\in K[t]
be an irreducible polynomial.
If
\alpha
is a root of
f(t)=0
, no integral shift of
\alpha
is a root.

Proof.

Set
f(t)=a_{0}+\cdots+a_{N-1}t^{N-1}+t^{N}
and fix an integer
m\neq 0
such that
f(\alpha+m)=0
.
We prove that the existence of
m
leads to a contradiction.
A swift computation shows that

\displaystyle 0=f(\alpha+m)=f(\alpha)+(\text{terms of order }\leq N-1\text{ in }\alpha):=f(\alpha)+g(\alpha)=g(\alpha)\,,

(410)

where in the last step we used that
\alpha
is a root of
f
.
The coefficient of
\alpha^{N-1}
in
g(\alpha)
is
Nm
, which is clearly non-zero.
This means that
g(\alpha)=0
, i.e.
\alpha
is also a root of
g(t)
.
But this is a contradiction, because
f(t)
is the minimal polynomial
35
35

35

The minimal polynomial of
\alpha
over
K
is the polynomial with the
smallest degree, whose roots contain
\alpha
.

.

\Box

Suppose we are given a pair of irreducible polynomials
f_{1},f_{2}\in K[t]
.
If they share a root
\alpha
modulo
\mathbb{Z}
, that means that
f_{1}(t+s)=f_{2}(t)
has an integral solution as a system of equations in
s
.
This is seen by expanding both sides and collecting powers of
t
, leading to a polynomial system for
s
of the form
g_{0}(s)=\ldots=g_{N-1}(s)=0
.
The GCD
g(s)
is used whether we have an integral solution or not.

Let us recall a well-known fact from linear algebra (see e.g.
(
Dummit-Foote-PID-Jordan
, Chapter 12)
).
Given an
r\times r
matrix
P
with entries in
K
, we can equip
K^{r}
with the structure of a
K[t]
-module by letting
t
act on
K^{r}
via left multiplication by
P
.
Consider an exact sequence

K[t]^{r}\quad\overset{t\mathbb{1}_{r}-P}{\longrightarrow}\quad K[t]^{r}\quad\longrightarrow\quad K^{r}\quad\longrightarrow\quad 0\,.

(411)

The last morphism sends
f(t)\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
to
f(P)\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
, where
f(t)\in K[t]
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
is the standard unit vector.
The fundamental theorem of principal ideal domains shows that
t\mathbb{1}_{r}-P
is transformed into a canonical form

\displaystyle L(t)(t\mathbb{1}_{r}-P)R(t)=\texttt{Diag[}\,1,\,\ldots,\,1,\,d_{1}(t),\,\ldots,\,d_{p}(t)\,\texttt{]}

(412)

for some invertible polynomial matrices
L(t),R(t)\in GL(r,K[t])
with
d_{1}(t)|\cdots|d_{p}(t)
.
According to the Chinese remainder theorem, we thus have an isomorphism (which can be made algorithmically explicit)

\displaystyle K^{r}\overset{\varphi}{\leftarrow}\ \bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}K[t]/d_{i}(t)\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{q_{i}}K[t]/d_{ij}(t)^{m_{ij}}

(413)

where
d_{i}(t)=\prod_{j=1}^{q_{i}}d_{ij}(t)^{m_{ij}}
is the irreducible decomposition.
The morphism
\varphi
sends any element
(f_{i}(t))_{i=1}^{p}\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}K[t]/d_{i}(t)
to
\sum_{i=1}^{p}f(t)v_{i}(t)|_{t\mapsto P}
where
v_{i}(t)
is the
(r-p+i)
-th column vector of
L(t)^{-1}
and the subscript
t\mapsto P
stands for the operation of replacing an expression
t^{a}v

(v\in K^{r})
by
P^{a}v
.
Each subspace
K[t]/d_{ij}(t)^{m_{ij}}
is invariant under multiplication by
t
, i.e. left multiplication by
P
.
This means that we can find a basis of
K^{r}
such that the left multiplication by
P
is block-diagonalized into a direct sum of multiplication by
t
on
K[t]/d_{ij}(t)^{m_{ij}}
.
It is not difficult to check that the
d_{ij}(t)
share a common root modulo
\mathbb{Z}
, as explained in the previous paragraph.
Finally, any basis of each block
K[t]/d_{ij}(t)^{m_{ij}}
is sent to a set of column vectors
B_{ij}
.
The matrix
Q
is obtained by aligning these
B_{ij}
to form a square matrix.

Remark B.7
.

In the examples of Pfaffian systems for Feynman integrals discussed in this paper, any eigenvalue of the residue matrix is an integral linear combination of
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\delta
.
Since the Euler integral (
2
) admits a meromorphic continuation as in
(
berkesch2014euler
, Theorem 2.5)
, we expect this to be true in general.

Appendix C
Improvements on restriction algorithms

Here we remark on subtle improvements to steps in the restriction algorithms presented in the main body of this text.

C.1
\mathcal{D}
-module-level restriction

The
b
-function played a central role in the
\mathcal{D}
-module restriction protocol of
Section
4.1
.
However, it is well-known that the
b
-function is computationally expensive to obtain.
When we can find a logarithmic connection for
{\cal D}^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}
via another method
(recall the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
below
Remark
4.3
), such as the Macaulay matrix method, we can utilize the following proposition to compute the
b
-function more easily:

Proposition C.1
.

Let
S=[s_{1}=1,s_{2},\ldots,s_{r}]^{T}
be a set of standard monomials for
\mathcal{I}

such that
\mathrm{ord}\,s_{i}=0
.
We denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}={\cal R}^{\prime}\mathcal{I}\cap{\cal D}^{\prime}
the Weyl closure of
\mathcal{I}
.
Assume that the Pfaffian system with respect to
S
has the form

\displaystyle\partial_{x}S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{P_{-1}}{x}+P_{0}+xP_{1}+\ldots\right)\cdot S\quad\mathrm{mod}\ {\cal R}^{\prime}\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\partial_{y}S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Q_{0}+xQ_{1}+\ldots\right)\cdot S\quad\mathrm{mod}\ {\cal R}^{\prime}\mathcal{I}

Then the eigenvalues of
P_{-1}
contain the roots of the
b
-function
of
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}

for the weight
w=(1,0)
.

Proof
.
We note that
x\partial_{x}S=P_{-1}\cdot S
modulo
F_{-1}+{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}
.
Here
F_{-1}
is the filtration defined in (
87
).
Let
\varphi(s)
be the characteristic polynomial of
P_{-1}
.
By the Cayley-Hamilton theorem, we have
\varphi(P_{-1})=0
.
Hence, we have
\varphi(x\partial_{x})S=0
modulo
F_{-1}+{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}
.
In particular, we have
\varphi(x\partial_{x})=0
modulo
F_{-1}+{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}

because
s_{1}=1
.
Therefore, the
b
-function is a factor of
\varphi(s)
.

\Box

If we have Pfaffian matrices for a logarithmic connection
with the singular locus
x=0
,
we can accelerate
Algorithm
4.4
with help from the residue matrix
P_{-1}
as follows.
Let
S
be a column vector consisting of standard monomials
of a Gröbner basis
of
\mathcal{I}
in
{\cal R}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}(x,y)\langle\partial_{x},\partial_{y}\rangle
.
Suppose that

x\partial_{x}S-(P_{-1}+P_{0}x+\ldots)\cdot S\equiv 0\quad\mathrm{mod}\ {\overline{\mathcal{I}}}

(414)

where
xP=P_{-1}+P_{0}x+\ldots
is the Pfaffian matrix in the
x
direction multiplied by
x
.
Taking the limit
x=0
in (
414
), we have

P_{-1}\cdot S\equiv 0\quad\mathrm{mod}\ {\overline{\mathcal{I}}}+x{\cal D}^{\prime}
,
which gives a relation among standard monomials modulo
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}+x{\cal D}^{\prime}
.

We can obtain higher-order relations by applying
\partial_{x}^{j}

to (
414
) and taking the limit
x=0
.
For example, applying
\partial_{x}
in conjunction with the relation
\partial_{x}S\equiv P\cdot S
,

\partial_{x}S+x\partial_{x}^{2}S\equiv\big(P_{-1}+O(x)\big)\cdot S+\big(P_{-1}+P_{0}x+O(x^{2})\big)\cdot x^{-1}\big(P_{-1}+P_{0}x+O(x^{2})\big)\cdot S\,.

(415)

When
P_{-1}\cdot S\equiv 0
, the limit
x=0
yields

\partial_{x}S\equiv P_{-1}\cdot S+(P_{-1}\cdot P_{0}+P_{0}\cdot P_{-1})\cdot S\equiv 0\quad\mathrm{mod}\ {\overline{\mathcal{I}}}+x{\cal D}^{\prime}\,.

(416)

Let
{\cal P}
be the set of the relations coming from
P_{-1}\cdot S\equiv 0
and higher-order obtained via the Pfaffian equation (
414
).

Algorithm C.2

(Rational restriction to
x=0
using the residue matrix
P_{-1}
)
.

Input
: Generators
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{\mu}\}
of a holonomic ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
{\cal D}^{\prime}
.
The relations
{\cal P}
.
Holonomic rank
r
of the restriction to
x=0
of
\mathcal{I}
.
A non-negative integer
\gamma
such that
\gamma\geq\max(s_{0}+1,s_{1}+1)
.

Output
: Generators of a left submodule
\mathcal{J}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}
of
{\cal D}^{\gamma}
such that

{\cal R}^{\gamma}/{\cal R}(\mathcal{J}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma})={\cal R}\otimes_{\cal D}{\cal D}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{I}+x{\cal D}^{\prime})
.

1:

w=(1,0)

2:

k=\gamma-1

3:

repeat

4:

\mathcal{J}={\cal D}\cdot\Bigl\{v_{k}\,\bigl(\,:\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i}:{\big|_{x=0}}\bigr)\Bigm|\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i})\leq m,1\leq i\leq N,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\Bigr\}

5:

\cup\ \bigl\{v_{k}(p)\bigm|p\in{\cal P},\mathrm{ord}\,_{(-w,w)}(p)\leq k\bigr\}

6:

k:=k+1

7:

until

\mathrm{rank}\bigl({\cal D}^{\gamma}/\mathcal{J}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}\bigr)=r

8:

return

\mathcal{J}

Theorem C.3
.

Algorithm
C.2
stops and gives the correct answer.

Proof
.
Rows of the Pfaffian system belong to the Weyl closure
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}=({\cal R}^{\prime}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}^{\prime}
.
Then the elements of
{\cal P}
belong to
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}+x{\cal D}^{\prime}
,
and this gives an expression for
{\cal R}\otimes_{\cal D}({\cal D}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{I}+x{\cal D}^{\prime})

by
Proposition
4.1
.
Combining this fact with the proof of
Theorem
4.5
,
we reach the desired conclusion.

\Box

Example C.4
.

To illustrate the difference between
Algorithm
4.4
and
Algorithm
C.2
, we consider the Feynman integral for the one-loop triangle with one internal mass (for comparison, see the example from
Section
5.5.1
).
The Lee-Pomeransky polynomial takes the form

\displaystyle g(z;x)

\displaystyle=z_{1}x_{1}+z_{2}x_{2}+z_{3}x_{3}+z_{4}x_{1}^{2}+z_{5}x_{1}x_{2}+z_{6}x_{1}x_{3}

(417)

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{2}=z_{3}=z_{4}=z_{5}=1\quad\text{and}\quad z_{6}\text{ generic}.

(418)

The associated GKZ system has the data

\displaystyle A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&1&1&1&1&1\\
1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&2&1&1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1\\
\end{array}\right]\quad,\quad\beta=[b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}]\,.

The GKZ case with generic
z_{1},\ldots,z_{6}
is of holonomic rank
3
.
The Feynman integral, subject to (
418
), has rank
2
.
We proceed to compute this drop in rank via a
\mathcal{D}
-module restriction.

To begin, choose the simplex
\sigma=[1,2,3,4]
.
The remaining variables are
z_{5}
and
z_{6}
, and we seek the restriction
z_{5}=1
.
Shifting
z_{5}\to z_{5}+1
, we can alternatively restrict
z_{5}=0
.
The generators of the GKZ system then read (see
(
Chestnov:2022alh
, Appendix A)
for how to rewrite generators given a simplex
\sigma
)

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({z}_{5}-{z}_{6}+1)\underline{{\partial}_{5}\partial_{6}}+{b}_{4}{\partial}_{5}-{b}_{3}\partial_{6}

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[(-{z}_{6}+1){z}_{5}-{z}_{6}+1\big]\underline{{\partial}_{5}\partial_{6}}+{z}_{6}(1-{z}_{6})\partial_{6}^{2}+({b}_{4}{z}_{5}+{b}_{4}){\partial}_{5}

\displaystyle+\big[(-{b}_{1}+{b}_{2}+{b}_{3}+2{b}_{4}-1){z}_{6}+2{b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-2{b}_{3}-2{b}_{4}+1\big]\partial_{6}

\displaystyle-{b}_{4}({b}_{3}-{b}_{1}+{b}_{2}+{b}_{4})

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-z_{5}({z}_{5}+1)\underline{{\partial}_{5}^{2}}-{z}_{6}{z}_{5}{\partial}_{5}\partial_{6}+\big[(-{b}_{1}+{b}_{2}+2{b}_{3}+{b}_{4}-1){z}_{5}+{b}_{1}-{b}_{4}\big]{\partial}_{5}+{b}_{3}{z}_{6}\partial_{6}

\displaystyle-{b}_{3}({b}_{3}-{b}_{1}+{b}_{2}+{b}_{4})

where we underlined the leading terms according to the weight
w=(1,0)
.
By the Macaulay matrix method, we find Pfaffian matrices
P_{5}
and
P_{6}
written in the basis

S=[\partial_{5},\partial_{6},1]^{T}
.
These matrices have denominators

{z}_{5}({z}_{5}+1)({z}_{6}-{z}_{5}-1)

and

{z}_{6}({z}_{6}-1)({z}_{6}-{z}_{5}-1)

respectively.
Since the Pfaffian system is logarithmic along
z_{5}=0
,
we may apply
Algorithm
C.2
with

x=z_{5}
and
y=z_{6}
.
The residue matrix at
x=0
is

\displaystyle P_{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}{b}_{1}-{b}_{4}&{b}_{3}{y}&{b}_{3}({b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-{b}_{3}-{b}_{4})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
\end{array}\right].

In
(x,y)
variables, the basis reads
S=[\partial_{y},\partial_{x},1]^{T}
.
The only non-zero element of
P_{-1}\cdot S
is thus

({b}_{1}-{b}_{4}){\partial}_{x}+{b}_{3}{y}{\partial}_{y}+{b}_{3}({b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-{b}_{3}-{b}_{4}).

(426)

Next we execute
Algorithm
C.2
with
k=1,\,\gamma=2
and the target holonomic rank
r=2
.
The operator (
426
) is included in
\mathcal{J}
.
The POT Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{J}
in
{\cal D}^{2}
is the set

\displaystyle\{

\displaystyle[0,\ y(1-{y})\partial_{{y}}^{2}+((-{b}_{1}+{b}_{2}+{b}_{3}+2{b}_{4}-1){y}+2{b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-{b}_{3}-2{b}_{4}+1)\partial_{{y}}

\displaystyle\ \quad+{b}_{4}({b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-{b}_{3}-{b}_{4})]^{T},

\displaystyle[{b}_{1}-{b}_{4},\ {b}_{3}{y}\partial_{{y}}+{b}_{3}({b}_{1}-{b}_{2}-{b}_{3}-{b}_{4})]^{T}\ \}.

This is a rank
2
system when
b_{1}-b_{4}\not=0
, wherefore the terminating condition for the algorithm is met.

Incidentally, the
b
-function (the indicial polynomial) along
x=0
is

{s}({s}-{b}_{1}+{b}_{4}-1)
.
When
s=1
, it is equal to
-(b_{1}-b_{4})
, which appears in the second element
of the Gröbner basis.

\blacksquare

C.2
Pfaffian-level restriction

Here we collect technical remarks on the computational side of the
restriction method based on equations (
3.2
,
3.2
)
of
Section
3.2
.

C.2.1
Details on the
M
-matrix

We start with a matrix-based argument that motivates the bottom-left

\mathbb{0}
-block in the gauge-transformed Pfaffian
matrix (
3.2
). Following the row-decomposition
of (
3.2
), let us focus on the first
r^{\prime}
columns of
M^{-1}
:

\displaystyle M^{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\>{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}V}&\>{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}\star}\end{array}\right]=\vbox{\hbox{
\parbox[c]{88.29788pt}{\includegraphics[]{figures/full-mat-brace2}}
}}\,.

The column block
V
satisfies the two identities

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle r\times r}=M\cdot M^{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}B}\\
\hline\cr{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}R}\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\>{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}V}&\>{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}\star}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\>{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}B}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}V}&\>\star\\
\hline\cr\>{\color[rgb]{0.3242,0.0156,0.4766}R}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5742,0.0938,0.0938}V}&\>\star\end{array}\right]\implies\begin{cases}B\cdot V=\mathbb{1}_{\scriptscriptstyle r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}}\>,\\
R\cdot V=\mathbb{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle(r-r^{\prime})\times r^{\prime}}\>.\end{cases}

Next, note that since the matrix
R
forms a basis for the row-span of

P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
, we can represent it as a product

R=T\cdot P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\>
, where
T
is some
(r-r^{\prime})\times r
matrix
(of row operations). If all non-zero rows of
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})

are already independent, we may choose
T
as a binary matrix, for
example

\displaystyle R=T\cdot P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdot}\end{array}\right]\cdot\vbox{\hbox{
\parbox[c]{84.02893pt}{\includegraphics[]{figures/square-mat}}
}}=\vbox{\hbox{
\parbox[c]{105.46301pt}{\includegraphics[]{figures/R-mat-brace}}
}}\>,

but in general
T
might be more complicated and depend on
z^{\prime}
.
We plug this decomposition into the LHS of (
3.2
) and focus
on its bottom block

\displaystyle\bigl(\partial_{i}R+R\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot M^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad=\bigl((\partial_{i}T)\cdot P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})+T\cdot[P_{i}(0,z^{\prime}),\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})]+T\cdot P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot M^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad=\bigl((\partial_{i}T)+T\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot\bigl(P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot M^{-1}\bigr)\>.

(436)

At this stage we recall that the second condition of (
C.2.1
)
implies

\displaystyle R\cdot V=\mathbb{0}\Longleftrightarrow T\cdot P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot V=\mathbb{0}\implies P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot V=\mathbb{0}\>,

(437)

so that the second factor in (
436
) indeed has a
\mathbb{0}
-block

\displaystyle P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot M^{-1}=P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\>V&\>\star\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\>\mathbb{0}&\>\star\end{array}\right]\>,

which leads to the
\mathbb{0}
-block in the bottom-left corner
of (
3.2
).

C.2.2
An algorithmic choice of the
B
-matrix

Using the notation of
Section
3.2
, it is easy to see that
one possible choice of the
r^{\prime}\times r
basis matrix
B
appearing in (
3.2
) is
simply the nullspace of the
r\times r
residue matrix
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle B:=\texttt{NullSpace[}\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\,\texttt{]}=\texttt{NullSpace[}\,R\,\texttt{]}\>,

(440)

where
R:=\texttt{RowReduce[}\,P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})\,\texttt{]}
is the matrix of independent
rows of
P_{1}(0,z^{\prime})
.
Now, recall that the restricted Pfaffian system is
actually an integrable connection on a space given as a quotient by the rows of
R

(see
Proposition
3.3
).
Hence, we are free to add any linear combination of rows from the
R
-matrix to simplify our choice of the
basis matrix
B
without changing the restricted Pfaffian system.
In practice, we can do such a simplification by row reducing the auxiliary
block matrix

\displaystyle\texttt{RowReduce\resizebox{}{652649}{[}}\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbb{1}_{r^{\prime}}&B\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{0}_{(r-r^{\prime})\times r^{\prime}}&R\end{array}\right]\,\texttt{\resizebox{}{652649}{]}}

and then extracting the top right
r^{\prime}\times r
block.

C.2.3
Inversion of the
M
-matrix

To find the restricted Pfaffian
Q_{i}
in (
3.2
) it is
enough to only partially invert the
M
-matrix (
3.2
).
Indeed, using the column decomposition (
444
)
to find
Q_{i}
we only need to determine the
V
-block
from the system (
C.2.1
) in two steps.
First find (any basis of) the right nullspace of
R

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}:=\texttt{NullSpace[}\,R\,\texttt{]}\,,

(443)

and then rotate it from the right to fix the normalization

\displaystyle V:=\widetilde{V}\cdot(B\cdot\widetilde{V})^{-1}\ .

(444)

This second step will require inversion of a smaller
r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}

matrix
(B\cdot\widetilde{V})
, which is easier than inverting
the full
r\times r
matrix
M
.

With the column block (
444
), the Pfaffian
Q_{i}
can be extracted from the full restriction gauge transformation as follows:

\displaystyle\bigl(\partial_{i}B+B\cdot P_{i}(0,z^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot V=Q_{i}\ .

(445)

Appendix D
Proofs

D.1
Theorem
2.1

Setting
\mathcal{O}(X):=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m},x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{n}^{\pm 1},\frac{1}{g}]
, we define an action of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
on

f=f(z,x)\in\mathcal{O}(X)
by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\bullet f

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{i}}+\beta_{0}\left({1\over g(z;x)}{\partial g(z;x)\over\partial z_{i}}\right)f\ ,

(446)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\bullet f

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}+\beta_{0}\left({1\over g(z;x)}{\partial g(z;x)\over\partial x_{i}}\right)f-\beta_{i}\frac{f}{x_{i}}\ .

(447)

The symbol

\mathcal{O}(X)\,g^{\beta_{0}}x_{1}^{-\beta_{1}}\dots x_{n}^{-\beta_{n}}
denotes the left
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{O}(X)
endowed with this action.
By definition,
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
is the
0
-th cohomology group of the direct image
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module
\int_{\pi}\mathcal{O}(X)\,g^{\beta_{0}}x_{1}^{-\beta_{1}}\dots x_{n}^{-\beta_{n}}
.
The first claim is an immediate consequence of the preservation of regular holonomicity
(
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
, Theorem 6.1.5)
.

Let us prove the second claim.
Since
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
is holonomic, it is an integrable connection on a generic open subset of
Y
.
We fix a generic
z\in Y
.
Then the restriction of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)
to
z
is the
n
-th twisted cohomology group on
\pi^{-1}(z)
with its twist given by the integrand of (
2
).
In view of
(
Agostini:2022cgv
, Theorem 2.6)
, its dimension is given by
|\chi(\pi^{-1}(z))|
.

D.2
Theorem
2.6

Let
\varphi\in{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
.
Writing
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
for the algebraic closure of
\mathcal{K}
, the action of
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{K}
extends to that on
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
.
We can regard
\varphi
as an element of
{\rm End}_{\overline{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{R}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
where we set
\overline{\mathcal{M}}:=\overline{\mathcal{K}}\otimes_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{M}
.
We first prove that any eigenvalue of
\varphi
is in
\mathbb{C}
.
Let us take an eigenvector
v\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}
of
\varphi
.
Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is finite-dimensional over
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
, there exists a minimal
k
such that
{\rm Span}_{\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\{v,\partial_{1}v,\ldots,\partial_{1}^{k}v\}={\rm Span}_{\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\{v,\partial_{1}v,\ldots\}
.
It follows that there exist
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\partial_{1}^{k}v=\sum_{j=0}^{k}a_{j}\partial_{1}^{j}v
.
It is straightforward to see that

\varphi(\partial_{1}^{k}v)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}(\partial_{1}^{k-j}\alpha)\partial_{1}^{j}v=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{j}(\partial_{1}^{k-j}\alpha)\partial_{1}^{j}v+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a_{j}\alpha(\partial_{1}^{j}v)

(448)

holds true.
On the other hand, the fact that
\varphi
commutes with the action of
\partial_{1}
implies the following identity:

\varphi(\partial_{1}^{k}v)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a_{j}\varphi(\partial_{1}^{j}v)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}a_{j}\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}\binom{j}{\ell}(\partial_{1}^{j-\ell}\alpha)(\partial_{1}^{\ell}v).

(449)

Comparing the coefficients of
\partial_{1}^{k-1}v
in (
448
) and (
449
), we obtain that
\partial_{1}\alpha=0
.
Similarly, we can prove that
\partial_{i}\alpha=0
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Since such a function
\alpha
must be a constant function, it must belong to
\mathbb{C}
.
Now, suppose that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})\geq 2
and take a morphism
\varphi\in{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})
which is linearly independent from
{\rm id}_{\mathcal{M}}
over
\mathbb{C}
.
For any eigenvalue
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
of
\varphi
,
\alpha\cdot{\rm id}_{\mathcal{M}}-\varphi
has a non-trivial kernel, which is a non-trivial
\mathcal{R}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
. This is a contradiction.

D.3
Theorem
2.7

We set
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y}}M_{A}(\beta;Y)
.
Let
Y_{0}
be a non-empty Zariski open subset of
Y
.
Any
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-submodule
N
of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
gives rise to an
\mathcal{R}
-submodule
\mathcal{M}
.
Conversely, any
\mathcal{R}
-submodule
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{M}
is of the form
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}}N
for some non-empty Zariski open subset
Y_{0}
of
Y
and a
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-submodule
N
of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
.
Shrinking
Y_{0}
appropriately if necessary, we may assume that both
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
and
N
are free as
\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}
-modules, i.e., they are integrable connections.
Now, the sheaf
\mathcal{L}
of flat sections of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
admits an invariant Hermitian form.
This can be shown by adopting the argument of
(
goto2020homology
, p. 559-560)
since our exponent
\beta
is real and generic.
Then, the sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{N}
of flat sections of
N
admits an orthogonal complement
\mathcal{L}_{N^{\prime}}
which is the sheaf of flat sections of a
\mathcal{D}_{Y_{0}}
-submodule
N^{\prime}
of
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}
in view of
(
Deligne
, Théorème 5.9)
.
The direct sum decomposition
M_{A}(\beta;Y)|_{Y_{0}}=N\oplus N^{\prime}
gives rise to the corresponding decomposition
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, which proves that
\mathcal{M}
is a semi-simple
\mathcal{R}
-module.

D.4
Theorem
2.8

It is enough to show that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})>1
if
\mathcal{M}
is not simple.
By the assumption that
\mathcal{M}
is semi-simple, there exist a pair of
\mathcal{R}
-submodules
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
such that
\dim_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{N},\dim_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\geq 1
and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
The identity morphism
{\rm id}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}
extends uniquely to an
\mathcal{R}
-endomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
by the requirement
\varphi(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})=0
.
In the same way, the identity morphism
{\rm id}_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
extends to an
\mathcal{R}
-endomorphism
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
.
It is obvious from the construction that
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are linearly independent, which shows the desired inequality
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{M})>1
.

D.5
Proposition
3.4

In order to simplify the discussion, we only prove the case when
m=1\>
,
i.e. when
Y^{\prime}
is a single point and
Y\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}\>
. The
case of
m\geq 2
can be proved in the same way.

We identify
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
with the field of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
. It is
easy to see that the following morphism is well-defined:

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{r}/\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\ni[v]\mapsto[v]\in\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r}/(z_{1}\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r}+\mathcal{N})=\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}\>,

(450)

where the submodule
\mathcal{N}
is defined
in
Proposition
3.3
.
Let us take a constant column vector
v\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
and consider an
element of the form
v\,\partial_{1}^{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r}\>
for integer
n\geq 1
.
A straightforward computation shows the identity

v\,\partial_{1}^{n}\equiv\bigl(\mathbb{1}_{r}\,n-P_{1}(0)^{T}\bigr)^{-1}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\bigl(\partial_{1}^{k}P_{1}^{T}|_{z_{1}=0}\bigr)\cdot v\,\partial_{1}^{n-k}

in
\iota^{*}\mathcal{M}
, which proves that
\varphi
is surjective.
Finally, we prove that
\varphi
is injective. Let us assume that

\varphi([v])=[0]
.
Since the constant vector
v
does not contain
\partial_{1}\>
, there exist an element

p\in\mathcal{D}_{Y}^{r}
and a column vector
w\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
such that

v=z_{1}p+(\mathbb{1}_{r}\,z_{1}\partial_{1}-P_{1}(z_{1})^{T})\cdot w\>
.
Substituting
z_{1}=0
, we obtain
v=-P_{1}(0)^{T}\cdot w\>
, which proves that

[v]=[0]
.

D.6
Theorem
3.6

We prove the case
m=2
.
The proof can easily be generalized to the case of
m>2
.

Let
I(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}I_{k}(z_{2})\>z_{1}^{k}
be a vector of holomorphic
functions and let
P_{1}\>
,
P_{2}
be the
r\times r
Pfaffian matrices given by
(
39
). We expand
P_{1}
as shown in (
51
)
and
P_{2}
as

P_{2}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P_{2,k}(z_{2})\>z_{1}^{k}\>.

(451)

Then the integrability condition (
18
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\partial_{2}\,P_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=\bigl[P_{2,0},P_{1,-1}\bigr]\>,

\displaystyle\partial_{2}\,P_{1,k}-(k+1)P_{2,k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i\geq 0,\>j\geq-1\end{subarray}}\bigl[P_{2,i},P_{1,j}\bigr]\>.

(452)

The fact that
I(z_{1},z_{2})
is a solution of (
19
) is
equivalent to
\{I_{k}(z_{2})\}_{k}
being subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle\bigl((k+1)-P_{1,-1}\bigr)\cdot I_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,\>j\geq 0\end{subarray}}P_{1,i}\cdot I_{j}\>,\quad

for
k\geq-1\>
,

(453)

\displaystyle\partial_{2}I_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,\>j\geq 0\end{subarray}}P_{2,i}\cdot I_{j}\>,

for
k\geq 0\>
.

(454)

If
I(z_{1},z_{2})
is a solution of (
19
), it is clear that
I_{0}(z_{2})
is a solution to the restricted Pfaffian equation
(
52
).
Conversely, let
I_{0}(z_{2})
be a solution of the restricted Pfaffian equation
(
52
).
We can uniquely extend
I_{0}(z_{2})
to the solution

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}I_{k}(z_{2})\,z_{1}^{k}
by requiring that the coefficients

I_{k}(z_{2})
solve the linear system (
453
), that is:

\displaystyle\bigl((k+1)-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)\cdot I_{k+1}(z_{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,\>j\geq 0\end{subarray}}P_{1,i}(z_{2})\cdot I_{j}(z_{2})\>,

(455)

with
z_{2}
kept as a parameter. By assumption, the residue matrix
P_{1,-1}(z_{2})
is non-resonant, so the only integer eigenvalue it has is

0\>
, which means that the matrix on the LHS of (
455
) is
invertible and there is a unique
I_{k+1}(z_{2})
that solves it for each
k\geq-1\>
.
So by construction of (
455
), the condition (
453
)
is satisfied and it is left to show that the other condition (
454
) is
satisfied as well. To this end, consider the identity

\bigl((k+1)-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)\cdot\partial_{2}I_{k+1}(z_{2})=\bigl((k+1)-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)\ \cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k+1\\
i,\>j\geq 0\end{subarray}}P_{2,i}\cdot I_{j}(z_{2})\>,

(456)

which holds by induction in
k
from acting with
\partial_{2}
on (
455
)
and the integrability condition (
452
). Due to
invertibility of the common matrix factor here, this equation implies (
454
).

Finally, we prove the convergence of
I(z_{1},z_{2})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}I_{k}(z_{2})z_{1}^{n}
.
Let
f(t;z_{2})=\texttt{Det[}\,t-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\,\texttt{]}
be the characteristic polynomial of
P_{1,-1}
.
The leading coefficient of
f(t;z_{2})
in
t
is
1
, and thus there exists a
constant
C>0
such that
f(t;z_{2})\geq C\,t^{r}
for any
t>0
sufficiently large.
To be more precise,
z_{2}
belongs to a small region in which we have a
uniform bound on the coefficients of
f(t;z_{2})
. Therefore, the matrix norm of
the cofactor matrix of
\bigl(t-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)
can be estimated by
C^{\prime}\,t^{r-1}

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
and for any
t>0
sufficiently large. Taking into
account Clamer’s formula for matrix inverse, we obtain

\left\lVert\bigl(t-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)^{-1}\right\rVert\leq C_{1}\,t^{-1}

(457)

for any
t>0
sufficiently large.
Here
\left\lVert\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\right\rVert
stands for a matrix norm.
On the other hand, convergence of the expansion (
51
) and Cauchy’s
inequality implies that there are positive constants
C_{2}
and
s
such that

\left\lVert P_{1,k}(z_{2})\right\rVert\leq C_{2}\,s^{k}\>,

(458)

for any
k\geq 0
.
We claim that the inequality

\left\lVert I_{k}(z_{2})\right\rVert\leq R^{n}\>\left\lVert I_{0}(z_{2})\right\rVert

(459)

is true with
R=\max(C_{1}C_{2},s)\>
.
Indeed, if the estimate (
459
) is true up to
k
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert I_{k+1}(z_{2})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\bigl((k+1)-P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\bigr)^{-1}\right\rVert\>\sum_{i+j=k}\left\lVert P_{i}(z_{2})\right\rVert\>\left\lVert I_{j}(z_{2})\right\rVert

(460)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\,C_{2}\,\frac{1}{k+1}\sum_{i+j=k}s^{i}\,R^{j}\>\left\lVert I_{0}(z_{2})\right\rVert

(461)

\displaystyle\leq R^{k+1}\>\left\lVert I_{0}(z_{2})\right\rVert\>.

(462)

This proves the convergence of
I(z_{1},z_{2})\>
.
Uniqueness of
I
is clear from (
453
) and the assumption of the theorem.

D.7
Theorem
3.7

We use the same notation as in
Appendix
D.6
.
Suppose we construct a matrix
G(z_{1},z_{2})\in GL(r;\mathbb{C}\{\!\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\!\})
such that the gauge transformation
I(z_{1},z_{2})=G(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot J(z_{1},z_{2})
turns the system (
19
) into

\displaystyle\partial_{1}J(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma}{z_{1}}J(z_{1},z_{2})\>,

\displaystyle\partial_{2}J(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=0\>,

(463)

for some constant matrix
\Gamma\>
. This system can be solved by the
matrix exponential:
J(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}^{\Gamma}\>
.

The transformation matrix
G
is also subject to its own Pfaffian system:

\displaystyle\partial_{1}G

\displaystyle=T_{P_{1},\>\Gamma/z_{1}}(G)\>,

\displaystyle\partial_{2}G

\displaystyle=P_{2}\cdot G\>.

(464)

Here
T_{A,B}:M(r;\mathbb{C})\to M(r;\mathbb{C})
is a linear map defined by
T_{A,B}(G):=A\cdot G-G\cdot B\>
.
Note that this is a Pfaffian system of the rank
r^{2}\>
.

Lemma D.1
.

The system (
D.7
) is integrable.

Proof.

For a matrix
A\in M(r;\mathbb{C})
, let us denote by
T_{A}:M(r;\mathbb{C})\to M(r;\mathbb{C})
the
linear map given by the left multiplication with
A\>
, explicitly

T_{A}(G):=A\cdot G\>
, so that
T_{A}(G)=T_{A,\mathbb{0}}(G)\>
.
We want to prove the following integrability condition:

\partial_{1}T_{P_{2}}-\partial_{2}T_{P_{1},\>\Gamma/z_{1}}=\bigl[T_{P_{1},\>\Gamma/z_{1}},\>T_{P_{2}}\bigr]\>.

(465)

To show this, we use identities for the three building
blocks appearing above, namely for the two derivatives

\partial_{1}T_{P_{2}}=T_{\partial_{1}P_{2}}\>
and

\partial_{2}T_{P_{1},\>\Gamma/z_{1}}=T_{\partial_{2}P_{1}}\>
,
and for the commutator
\bigl[T_{P_{1},\>\Gamma/z_{1}},\>T_{P_{2}}\bigr]=T_{[P_{1},\>P_{2}]}\>
.
Combining them and taking into account the integrability
condition (
19
) proves the integrability of the
system (
D.7
).

\Box

The Pfaffian system (
D.7
) is logarithmic along
\{z_{1}=0\}
.

Theorem
3.6
shows that there exists a convergent solution
G

to (
D.7
) with
G(0,0)=\mathbb{1}_{r}\>
, since

\mathbb{1}_{r}\in\texttt{NullSpace[}\,{\rm Res}\bigl(T_{P_{1},\Gamma/z_{1}}\bigr)|_{z_{2}=0}\,\texttt{]}

is a valid boundary condition.
Therefore, any solution to the system (
19
) can be
written in the form

I(z_{1},z_{2})=G(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot z_{1}^{\Gamma}\cdot y=\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}G(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot\Gamma^{n}\cdot y\>\log^{n}z_{1}

(466)

for some
y\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
, which is fixed by the boundary condition.
This proves that the boundary value map
I(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto I_{0}(0,z_{2})
of
Theorem
3.7
is injective.
Let us prove that the boundary value map is surjective.
We observe that

P_{1,-1}(z_{2})\cdot G(0,z_{2})=G(0,z_{2})\cdot\Gamma

(467)

is true by (
D.7
).
This identity shows that any holomorphic solution
I(z_{2})
of
(
52
) can be written as
G(0,z_{2})\cdot y\>
, where

y\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
is an element of the generalized
0
-eigenspace of
\Gamma\>
.
So we conclude that
I(z_{1},z_{2})=G(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot z_{1}^{\Gamma}\cdot y
is the desired solution of
(
19
) with the boundary value
G(0,z_{2})\cdot y
.

Remark D.2
.

Finally let us comment on how the Pfaffian system (
D.7
)
can be constructed in practice.
If we choose to flatten the
G
matrix into a vector
\vec{G}
of length

r^{2}
as

\displaystyle\vec{G}:=\texttt{Flatten[}\,G\,\texttt{]}

(468)

in
Mathematica
notation,
then the matrix representation of the
T_{A,B}
map (which we denote by
the same symbol) becomes
36
36

36

In tensorial notation:
T_{A,B}=A\otimes\mathbb{1}-\mathbb{1}\otimes B^{T}\>
.

\displaystyle T_{A,B}=\texttt{Flatten[}\,\texttt{Outer[}\,\texttt{Times,}A\texttt{,}\mathbb{1}\,\texttt{]}-\texttt{Outer[}\,\texttt{Times,}\mathbb{1}\texttt{,}B^{T}\,\texttt{]}\texttt{,\lx@text@lbrace\lx@text@lbrace 1, 3\lx@text@rbrace, \lx@text@lbrace 2, 4\lx@text@rbrace\lx@text@rbrace}\,\texttt{]}\>.

(469)

For example, let

A=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{22.33946pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/mat1}}}}\right]
and

B=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{22.33946pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/mat2}}}}\right]
be
some
2\times 2
matrices,
then the
4\times 4
matrix
T_{A,B}
looks like the following difference:

\displaystyle T_{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{8.93579pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/mat1}}}}\right],\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{8.93579pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/mat2}}}}\right]}=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{43.67918pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/mat1oid}}}}\right]-\left[\vbox{\hbox{\parbox[c]{43.67918pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/idomat2t}}}}\right]\>.

(470)

So we conclude, that the rank
r^{2}
Pfaffian system (
D.7
) can be
obtained from the original system (
19
) by simple
matrix operations, and, as can be seen from (
470
), these two
Pfaffian systems share many common properties, such sparsity and
block-triangularity, which is important for practical computations.

D.8
Proposition
4.1

The map
{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}\ni\ell\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\mapsto}}\ell\in{\cal D}_{Y}/({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}

is surjective.
Then the induced map

\varphi\,:\,{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})\longrightarrow{\cal D}_{Y}/(({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})

is surjective, and the induced map for solution spaces

\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{Y}}({\cal D}_{Y}/({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y},{\cal O}(U))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{Y}}({\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I},{\cal O}(U))

is injective for a sufficiently small open ball
U
.
When
U
is a simply connected domain outside of the singular locus of
{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
,
holomorphic solutions on
U
of the two systems

{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
and
{\cal D}_{Y}/({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}
agree.
Therefore, the injective map
\psi
is an isomorphism for any sufficiently
small open ball
U

by the identity theorem of complex function theory.
Let
U
be a small open ball with a center on

Y^{\prime}=\{z\,|\,z_{1}=\ldots=z_{m^{\prime}}=0\}

and suppose that the center is outside of the singular locus of
the restriction
{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})
.
Then we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{Y}}({\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I},{\cal O}(U))|_{V}=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}({\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y}),{\cal O}(U\cap V))
.
It implies that the holonomic ranks
of

{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})

and

{\cal D}_{Y}/(({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})

agree.
The left
{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}
morphism induced by
\varphi
from

{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})

to

{\cal R}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}}{\cal D}_{Y}/(({\cal R}_{Y}\mathcal{I})\cap{\cal D}_{Y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}z_{j}{\cal D}_{Y})

is surjective, and the vector space dimensions of these two agree
over the field
\mathbb{C}(z_{{m^{\prime}}+1},\ldots,z_{m})
.
Thus,
\varphi
is one-to-one.

D.9
Theorem
4.5

Let
r
be the rank of the restriction.
Recall that the
(-1,0,1,0)
-order of the monomial

\ell=x^{\alpha_{1}}y^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}^{\beta_{2}}

is
-\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}
.
We denote it by
\mathrm{ord}\,(\ell)=-\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}
, thereby omitting the suffix
(-w,w)
.

Since the input is holonomic, there exists an operator of the form

B=b(\theta_{x})+xc(x,y,\theta_{x},\partial_{y}),\quad\theta_{x}=x\partial_{x},\ b(u)\in\mathbb{C}[s],\ c\in{\cal D}^{\prime}

(471)

in
\mathcal{I}
(see, e.g.,
(
SST
, Theorem 5.1.2)
).
Note that
\mathrm{ord}\,(b(\theta_{x}))=0
and

\mathrm{ord}\,(xc(x,y,\theta_{x},\partial_{y}))<0
.
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{k}B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(\theta_{x}+k)\partial_{x}^{k}+\partial_{x}^{k}xc(x,y,\theta_{x},\partial_{y})

(472)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b(k)\partial_{x}^{k}+(b(\theta_{x}+k)-b(k))\partial_{x}^{k}+\partial_{x}^{k}xc(x,y,\theta_{x},\partial_{y}).

We have

(b(\theta_{x}+k)-b(k))|_{x=0}=0
,
and

\mathrm{ord}\,(\partial_{x}^{k}xc(y,\theta_{x},\partial_{y})<k
.
Then the equation (
472
) implies that

b(k)\partial_{x}^{k}=0
modulo
x{\cal D}^{\prime}+\mathcal{I}+F_{k-1}

where
F_{k-1}
is defined in (
87
).
This property, which was firstly utilized for the restriction algorithm in
Oaku-1997
, is a key in proving the termination
of the algorithm
(see also
(
SST
, Lemma 5.1.8)
).

The operators
f_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
are generators of the left ideal
\mathcal{I}
.
Suppose that the operator
B
is expressed as

B=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{m_{i}}d_{ij}(x,y,\partial_{y})\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i}\,.

Let
u_{0}
be the maximal non-negative integral root of
b(u)=0
.
We denote by
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
the standard basis of
{\cal D}^{k+1}
.
In other words,
e_{i}
stands for
\partial_{x}^{i}
, and we use this identification in this proof.
Note that the last element of
v_{k}(\ell)
is the component for
e_{0}
,
the second to last element of
v_{k}(\ell)
is the component for
e_{1}
,
and so on.
When
k\geq\mathrm{max}(u_{0}+1,\mathrm{max}\,(m_{i}+\mathrm{ord}\,(f_{i})))=:k_{0}
,
it follows from (
472
) that the left module
generated by

v_{k}(:\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i}:{|_{x=0}}),\quad\mathrm{ord}\,(\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i})\leq k,\quad i=1,\ldots,N,\quad j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(473)

in
{\cal D}^{k+1}

contains an element of the form

b(k^{\prime})e_{k^{\prime}}+\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}-1}d_{i}(y,\partial_{y})e_{i},\quad k^{\prime}>u_{0},b(k^{\prime})\not=0\,.

(474)

Let
G_{k}
be a Gröbner basis by the POT order
of

\Bigl\{v_{k}\,\bigl(\,:\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i}:{\big|_{x=0}}\bigr)\Bigm|1\leq i\leq N,\mathrm{ord}\,(\partial_{x}^{j}f_{i})\leq k,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\Bigr\}

(475)

in
{\cal D}^{k+1}
.
The element (
474
) is contained in
G_{k}

when
k\geq k_{0}
, which means that
there is no standard monomial of the form
e_{k^{\prime}}
,
k^{\prime}>u_{0}

in the Gröbner basis.
We note that the submodules

{\cal D}G_{k}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}
represent an increasing set,
i.e. we have

{\cal D}G_{k}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}\subseteq{\cal D}G_{k+1}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}
.
For a sufficiently large
k
, the submodule

{\cal D}G_{k}\cap{\cal D}^{\gamma}
contains the denominator module of (
89
)
because the
v_{k+1}(:\partial{x}^{\beta}g_{i}:{|_{x=1}})
’s of
g_{i}
in (
89
)
are contained in
{\cal D}G_{k}
.
Note that
e_{i}
for
u_{0}<i\leq\gamma
do not appear in the standard monomials.

Recall the restriction algorithm of
{\cal D}
-modules (see
Section
4.1
and
(
SST
, Section 5.2)
).
The algorithm
constructs the restriction by the
(-w,w)
-Gröbner basis
G
.
Elements for which the
(-w,w)
-order is more than
u_{0}
are not used
to construct the restriction.
Our procedure does not have this trashing procedure, so our module

{\cal D}G_{k}
might contain these trashed elements.
This is why we need to take
\gamma
larger than
u_{1}

(see also
Example
4.8
).

Consider two left
{\cal R}
-submodules
{\cal R}J
and
{\cal R}J^{\prime}
of
{\cal R}^{\gamma}

such that
{\cal R}J\subset{\cal R}J^{\prime}
.
If
\dim_{\mathbb{C}(y)}{\cal R}^{\gamma}/({\cal R}J)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}(y)}{\cal R}^{\gamma}/({\cal R}J^{\prime})
,
then these two
{\cal R}
modules are isomorphic.
Thus, we obtain the conclusion.

\Box

As final remark, note that the identity
(
472
) is the key to proving the existence of
\gamma
.

D.10
Lemma
4.9

Consider the holomorphic solution sheaf

\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{N},{\cal O}^{an})
on
Y^{\prime}
.
The germs are finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
;
let
r
be the their maximal dimension.
Let
W^{\prime}
be the maximal subset of
Y^{\prime}
such that
the dimension of the vector space is
r
.
Since the solution sheaf is a perverse sheaf
(
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
, Section 4.6)
, the codimension of
W^{\prime}

in
Y^{\prime}
is
0
.
Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
be linearly independent holomorphic solutions on
W^{\prime}
.
Consider the matrix
H=(s_{i}\bullet h_{j})

under the assumption
0\in W^{\prime}
.
Since
S=\{s_{i}\}
is the basis of
{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}{\cal D}_{Y})
,
the determinant
|H|
is not identically equal to
0

(
SST
, Proposition 5.2.14)
.
Let
W
be the non-zero locus of
|H|
in
W^{\prime}
.
We may suppose that
0
is in
W
from the beginning.
Let
\partial_{Y^{\prime}}
be an element of
\mathcal{D}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
Note that the restriction
\mathcal{N}
is regular holonomic.
Then
H^{-1}\partial_{Y^{\prime}}\bullet H
is a matrix consisting of regular function on
W
,
and it gives the action of
\partial_{Y^{\prime}}
to
S
.

\Box

D.11
Theorem
4.14

Let
\mathbb{C}(Y^{\prime})
be the fraction field of
the quotient ring
\mathbb{C}[Y^{\prime}]=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}]/L
.
We note that the following identities hold,

{\cal D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+L{\cal D}_{Y})\simeq{\cal D}_{Y}/L{\cal D}_{Y}\otimes_{{\cal D}_{Y}}{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}\simeq\mathbb{C}[Y^{\prime}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I},\

(476)

and regular functions on
Y^{\prime}
can be expressed by elements of
\mathbb{C}[Y^{\prime}]
.
This is holonomic
{\cal D}_{Y^{\prime}}
-module and is isomorphic to
\mathcal{N}=\iota^{*}\mathcal{D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}
.
Since the support of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}/(\mathcal{I}+L\mathcal{D}_{Y})

is
Y^{\prime}=V(L)
, it can also be regarded as the left
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
-module on
Y

supported on
Y^{\prime}

by the Kashiwara equivalence (see, e.g.,
(
Hotta-Tanisaki-Takeuchi-2008
, Section 1.6)
)
and then
\partial_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
act on it.
Since
S
is the basis of the connection
\mathcal{N}(W)

on the maximal dimensional stratum
W
,

1\times s_{j}
’s can be regaded as the basis of

\mathbb{C}(Y^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}\mathbb{C}[Y^{\prime}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}\simeq\mathbb{C}(Y^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[z]}{\cal D}_{Y}/\mathcal{I}

as a vector space over
\mathbb{C}(Y^{\prime})
.
Then there exists a matrix
P_{i}(z)\in\mathbb{C}(Y^{\prime})^{r\times r}

such that

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\times\partial_{i}s_{1}\\
\vdots\\
1\times\partial_{i}s_{r}\\
\end{array}\right]=P_{i}(z)\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\times s_{1}\\
\vdots\\
1\times s_{r}\\
\end{array}\right].

(477)

Thus, we obtain the conclusion.

\Box

D.12
Construction of a stratification by a comprehensive Gröbner basis

Here we explain how the stratum
W^{\prime}
, or a somewhat smaller one, can be obtained by
the comprehensive Gröbner basis

weispfenning-1992
.
Comprehensive Gröbner bases and an application to

{\cal D}
-module restrictions
are discussed in
nakayama-takayama-2023
.
A different approach to construct strata is given in
walther-2005
.

Let
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{\mu}\}
be a set of generators for a holonomic ideal in
{\cal D}_{Y}
.
Taking
n
indeterminates
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}
, we make a change of coordinates

z_{i}\rightarrow z_{i}+c_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,m

(478)

in every
f_{j}
.
We apply the
Algorithm
4.7
using the input

f_{1}(c,z,\partial),\ldots,f_{\mu}(c,z,\partial)
.
We make the translation to row echelon form
or compute the Gröbner basis with the POT order over the rational function
field
\mathbb{C}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
.
Let
g(c)
be the least common multiple of all the denominator polynomials needed
to obtain row echelon form.
Consider the stratum
V(L)\cap\{z\,|\,g(z)\not=0\}
.
The stratum is maximal dimensional.
Specializing
c
to a number vector in the stratum
for the parametric echelon form,
we obtain the echelon form to evaluate the rank.
It follows from the assumption on
g
that the rank is constant
on the stratum and maximal.
This is the first step in constructing the comprehensive
Gröbner basis.
